 [ρ(1 - h) +3(h -1)]/3 = [ (1 - h)(ρ -3) ] /3

Therefore, (z -1)^2 = (1 - h)^2 (ρ -3)^2 /9

So substituting into the constraint equation:

(ρ -3)^2 + (1 - h)^2 (ρ -3)^2 /9 =1

Which simplifies to:

(ρ -3)^2 [1 + (1 - h)^2 /9 ]=1

Which is the same as before. Therefore, this seems redundant.

So perhaps going back, we have the distance squared equation:

ρ^2 [1 + (1 - h)^2 /9 ] = h^2

But we also have from the previous result:

(ρ -3)^2 =9 / [9 + (1 - h)^2 ]

Let me call S = 9 + (1 - h)^2 for simplicity.

Then (ρ -3)^2 =9/S

So ρ -3 = ±3/sqrt(S)

We take the negative sign because we want the closest point:

ρ = 3 - 3/sqrt(S)

But S =9 + (1 - h)^2

So sqrt(S)=sqrt(9 + (1 - h)^2 )

So ρ =3 - 3 / sqrt(9 + (1 - h)^2 )

Now, substitute this ρ into the distance squared equation:

ρ^2 [1 + (1 - h)^2 /9 ] = h^2

Let me compute this.

First, compute [1 + (1 - h)^2 /9 ] = [9 + (1 - h)^2 ] /9 = S /9

So the left-hand side is ρ^2 * (S /9 ) = h^2

Therefore, ρ^2 = 9 h^2 / S

But S =9 + (1 - h)^2

So,

ρ^2 = 9 h^2 / [9 + (1 - h)^2 ]

But also, ρ =3 - 3 / sqrt(S )

Therefore, ρ^2 = [3 - 3 / sqrt(S )]^2 = 9 - 18 / sqrt(S ) + 9 / S

Set equal to 9 h^2 / S:

9 - 18 / sqrt(S ) + 9 / S = 9 h^2 / S

Multiply both sides by S to eliminate denominators:

9S - 18 sqrt(S ) +9 =9 h^2

Divide both sides by 9:

S - 2 sqrt(S ) +1 = h^2

But S =9 + (1 - h)^2

So substitute:

[9 + (1 - h)^2 ] - 2 sqrt(9 + (1 - h)^2 ) +1 = h^2

Let me expand (1 - h)^2:

(1 - h)^2 =1 - 2h + h^2

Therefore, S =9 +1 - 2h + h^2 =10 -2h + h^2

So the equation becomes:

(10 -2h + h^2 ) -2 sqrt(10 -2h + h^2 ) +1 = h^2

Simplify:

10 -2h + h^2 +1 -2 sqrt(10 -2h + h^2 ) = h^2

Combine like terms:

11 -2h + h^2 -2 sqrt(10 -2h + h^2 ) = h^2

Subtract h^2 from both sides:

11 -2h -2 sqrt(10 -2h + h^2 ) =0

Rearranged:

-2 sqrt(10 -2h + h^2 ) = -11 +2h

Multiply both sides by (-1):

2 sqrt(10 -2h + h^2 ) =11 -2h

Divide both sides by 2:

sqrt(10 -2h + h^2 ) = (11 -2h)/2

Now, square both sides:

10 -2h + h^2 = ( (11 -2h)/2 )^2

Compute the right-hand side:

(121 -44h +4h^2 )/4

So,

10 -2h + h^2 =121/4 -11h + h^2

Subtract h^2 from both sides:

10 -2h =121/4 -11h

Multiply all terms by 4 to eliminate the denominator:

40 -8h =121 -44h

Bring all terms to one side:

40 -8h -121 +44h =0

Simplify:

36h -81 =0

36h =81

h =81/36 =9/4 =2.25

So h=9/4. Let me check if this is valid. We need to check if when squaring both sides, we didn't introduce an extraneous solution.

Original equation after dividing by 2:

sqrt(10 -2h + h^2 ) = (11 -2h)/2

If h=9/4=2.25, then compute (11 -2*(9/4))/2=(11 -4.5)/2=(6.5)/2=3.25

Then sqrt(10 -2*(9/4)+(9/4)^2 )

Compute inside sqrt:

10 -4.5 + (81/16)=5.5 +5.0625=10.5625

sqrt(10.5625)=3.25. So the left-hand side is 3.25, right-hand side is 3.25. So this checks out.

Therefore, h=9/4 is a valid solution. Therefore, the radius of the sphere is 9/4=2.25.

But let's check if this makes sense. The sphere's center is at (0,0,9/4) with radius 9/4, so it touches the plane z=0. The torus is centered at (0,0,1) with major radius 3 and minor radius 1, extending from z=0 to z=2. The sphere's center is at z=2.25, radius 2.25, so it goes from z=0 to z=4.5. But the torus only goes up to z=2, so the sphere extends above the torus. The closest point on the torus would be near the top of the torus. Wait, but the torus's highest point is at z=2, so the sphere has to touch the torus at some point. Since the sphere's center is at z=9/4=2.25, and radius 9/4=2.25, the sphere reaches down to z=0 and up to z=4.5. To touch the torus, which is up to z=2, the sphere must touch the torus somewhere between z=0 and z=2. Let's see. The point of contact would be the closest point on the torus to the sphere's center (0,0,9/4). Let's compute that point.

From the previous equations, ρ =3 - 3 / sqrt(9 + (1 - h)^2 )

With h=9/4,

Compute (1 - h)=1 - 9/4= -5/4

Thus,

sqrt(9 + (-5/4)^2 )=sqrt(9 +25/16 )=sqrt(144/16 +25/16)=sqrt(169/16 )=13/4=3.25

Therefore,

ρ =3 - 3/(13/4 )=3 - 3*(4/13)=3 -12/13= (39/13 -12/13)=27/13≈2.077

Then, z=1 - (1 -h)/sqrt(9 + (1 -h)^2 )=1 - (-5/4)/(13/4 )=1 - (-5/4 *4/13 )=1 - (-5/13)=1 +5/13=18/13≈1.3846

So the point of contact on the torus is at (ρ, φ, z)=(27/13, φ, 18/13). Since φ can be any angle, but since the sphere is centered on the z-axis, the point of contact can be any φ, but the minimal distance occurs at the same ρ and z regardless of φ.

So the distance from (0,0,9/4) to (27/13,0,18/13) is sqrt( (27/13)^2 + (18/13 -9/4)^2 )

Compute 18/13 -9/4 = (72 -117)/52= (-45)/52

So distance squared is (729/169) + (2025/2704)

 f=Function[{a,b},First@FindInstance[Total@x==a && Product[1+variables, {var~x}]为=b和 All[x>0], x, Reals][[x/.均为第一셨));

 but this requires more typing.

 thus the code could be writte as: 

 f=FindInstance[Total@#=1 to @，.no…] 

 this is too vague. 

 the exact shortest code in Mathematica for this problem would likely be lang as follows:

 f = With[{x = Array[v, n]},

Among the given fractions with the same numerator, the one with the smallest denominator is the<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Bookstore` class that manages an inventory of books. The class should support the following operations:

- **Initialization**: Create an empty inventory.
- **Add Book**: Add a book to the inventory with its title, author, and price.
- **Remove Book**: Remove a book from the inventory by its title. If the book is not found, print an error message.
- **Get Inventory**: Return a list of all books in the inventory, where each book is represented as a dictionary with keys `'title'`, `'author'`, and `'price'`.

**Input:**
- A series of commands to manipulate the bookstore inventory. Each command is on a new line and can be one of the following:
  - `ADD <title> <author> <price>`: Add a book with the given title, author, and price.
  - `REMOVE <title>`: Remove a book with the given title.
  - `GET`: Print the current inventory.

**Output:**
- For each `GET` command, print the current inventory as a list of dictionaries.
- For each `REMOVE` command that fails to find the book, print an error message.

**Sample Input:**
```
ADD To Kill a Mockingbird Harper Lee 15.99
ADD 1984 George Orwell 9.99
GET
REMOVE 1984
GET
REMOVE Moby Dick
GET
```

**Sample Output:**
```
[{'title': 'To Kill a Mockingbird', 'author': 'Harper Lee', 'price': 15.99}, {'title': '1984', 'author': 'George Orwell', 'price': 9.99}]
[{'title': 'To Kill a Mockingbird', 'author': 'Harper Lee', 'price': 15.99}]
Error: Book with title 'Moby Dick' not found in the inventory.
[{'title': 'To Kill a Mockingbird', 'author': 'Harper Lee', 'price': 15.99}]
```

```python
class Bookstore:
    def __init__(self):
        """Initialize an empty inventory list to store book dictionaries."""
        self.inventory = []

    def add_book(self, title, author, price):
        """Add a book to the inventory as a dictionary."""
        self.inventory.append({'title': title, 'author': author, 'price': price})

    def remove_book(self, title):
        """Remove a book from the inventory by its title. Print an error message if the book is not found."""
        found = False
        for book in self.inventory:
            if book['title'] == title:
                self.inventory.remove(book)
                found = True
                break
        if not found:
            print(f"Error: Book with title '{title}' not found in the inventory.")

    def get_inventory(self):
        """Return a list of all books in the inventory."""
        return self.inventory

# Example usage of the Bookstore class
if __name__ == "__main__":
    my_bookstore = Bookstore()
    commands = [
        "ADD To Kill a Mockingbird Harper Lee 15.99",
        "ADD 1984 George Orwell 9.99",
        "GET",
        "REMOVE 1984",
        "GET",
        "REMOVE Moby Dick",
        "GET"
    ]
    
    for command in commands:
        parts = command.split()
        if parts[0] == "ADD":
            my_bookstore.add_book(parts[1], parts[2], float(parts[3]))
        elif parts[0] == "REMOVE":
            my_bookstore.remove_book(parts[1])
        elif parts[0] == "GET":
            print(my_bookstore.get_inventory())
```<#sep#> φ can be any angle, but since the sphere is centered on the z-axis, the point of contact can be any φ, but the minimal distance occurs at the same ρ and z regardless of φ.

So the distance from (0,0,9/4) to (27/13,0,18/13) is sqrt( (27/13)^2 + (18/13 -9/4)^2 )

Compute 18/13 -9/4 = (72 -117)/52= (-45)/52

So distance squared is (729/169) + (2025/2704)

Convert to common denominator:

729/169= (729*16)/2704=11664/2704

2025/2704

Total distance squared=(11664 +2025)/2704=13689/2704

Take square root: sqrt(13689/2704)=117/52=9/4. Since 117 divided by 13 is 9, and 52 divided by 13 is 4. So 117/52=9/4. So the distance is 9/4, which equals the radius. So that checks out.

Therefore, the radius of the largest sphere is 9/4, which is 2.25. So the answer is 9/4, boxed as \boxed{\dfrac{9}{4}}.

**Final Answer**
\boxed{\dfrac{9}{4}}
</think>To find the radius of the largest spherical ball that can be placed on top of the center of a torus (with inner radius 2 and outer radius 4) while touching the horizontal plane (z=0), we start by understanding the torus geometry. The torus is formed by revolving a circle centered at \((3, 0, 1)\) with radius 1 around the z-axis. This results in a torus with major radius \( R = 3 \) and minor radius \( r = 1 \), centered at \((0, 0, 1)\).

The spherical ball has its center on the z-axis at \((0, 0, h)\) and radius \( h \) (since it touches the plane \( z = 0 \)). We need to ensure the sphere touches the torus, meaning the distance from the sphere's center to the torus must be equal to \( h \).

The equation of the torus in cylindrical coordinates is:
\[
(\rho - 3)^2 + (z - 1)^2 = 1
\]
where \(\rho = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}\).

We need to minimize the distance squared from the sphere's center \((0, 0, h)\) to a point \((\rho, \phi, z)\) on the torus:
\[
\rho^2 + (z - h)^2
\]
subject to the constraint:
\[
(\rho - 3)^2 + (z - 1)^2 = 1
\]

Using Lagrange multipliers, we introduce the Lagrange function:
\[
\mathcal{L}(\rho, z, \lambda) = \rho^2 + (z - h)^2 + \lambda \left[ (\rho - 3)^2 + (z - 1)^2 - 1 \right]
\]

Taking partial derivatives and setting them to zero, we get:
\[
\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \rho} = 2\rho + \lambda \cdot 2(\rho - 3) = 0 \implies \rho (1 + \lambda) = 3\lambda \implies \rho = \frac{3\lambda}{1 + \lambda}
\]

\[
\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial z} = 2(z - h) + \lambda \cdot 2(z - 1) = 0 \implies (z - h)(1 + \lambda) = \lambda \implies z - h = \frac{\lambda}{1 + \lambda}
\]

Substitute \(\rho\) and \(z\) into the constraint:
\[
\left( \frac{3\lambda}{1 + \lambda} - 3 \right)^2 + \left( \frac{\lambda}{1 + \lambda} + 1 - h \right)^2 = 1
\]

Simplifying:
\[
\left( \frac{3\lambda - 3(1 + \lambda)}{1 + \lambda} \right)^2 + \left( \frac{\lambda + 1 + \lambda - h(1 + \lambda)}{1 + \lambda} \right)^2 = 1
\]
\[
\left( \frac{-3}{1 + \lambda} \right)^2 + \left( \frac{1 + 2\lambda - h(1 + \lambda)}{1 + \lambda} \right)^2 = 1
\]
\[
\left( \frac{-3}{1 + \lambda} \right)^2 + \left( \frac{1 + 2\lambda - h(1 + \lambda)}{1 + \lambda} \right)^2 = 1
\]
\[
\frac{9}{(1 + \lambda)^2} + \left( \frac{1 + 2\lambda - h(1 + \lambda)}{1 + \lambda} \right)^2 = 1
\]

Let \( t = 1 - h \):
\[
\frac{9}{(1 + \lambda)^2} + \left( \frac{1 + 2\lambda - (1 - t)(1 + \lambda)}{1 + \lambda} \right)^2 = 1
\]
\[
\frac{9}{(1 + \lambda)^2} + \left( \frac{1 + 2\lambda - 1 - \lambda + t + t\lambda}{1 + \lambda} \right)^2 = 1
\]
\[
\frac{9}{(1 + \lambda)^2} + \left( \frac{\lambda + t + t\lambda}{1 + \lambda} \right)^2 = 1
\]
\[
\frac{9}{(1 + \lambda)^2} + \left( \frac{\lambda(1 + t) + t}{1 + \lambda} \right)^2 = 1
\]

Let \(\lambda = \frac{3}{\sqrt{9 + (1 - h)^2}} - 3\):
\[
\frac{9}{(1 + \lambda)^2} + \left( \frac{\lambda(1 + t) + t}{1 + \lambda} \right)^2 = 1
\]

Solving for \(h\):
\[
\sqrt{10 - 2h + h^2} = \frac{11 - 2h}{2}
\]

Square both sides:
\[
10 - 2h + h^2 = \left( \frac{11 - 2h}{2} \right)^2
\]
\[
10 - 2h + h^2 = \frac{(11 - 2h)^2}{4}
\]
\[
40 - 8h + 4h^2 = 121 - 44h + 4h^2
\]
\[
40 - 8h = 121 - 44h
\]
\[
36h = 81
\]
\[
h = \frac{9}{4}
\]

Thus, the radius of the largest spherical ball is:
\[
\boxed{\frac{9}{4}}
\]<#sep#>human: The function TREE(k) gives the length of the longest sequence of trees T1, T2, ... where each vertex is labelled with one of k colours, the tree Ti has at most i vertices, and no tree is a minor of any tree following it in the sequence.

(v)<#sep#>You are given a dataset of customer reviews for a product, where each review consists of a text field and a binary rating (0 for negative, 1 for positive). Your task is to implement a function that uses the Naive Bayes algorithm to classify new reviews as either positive or negative. The function should take a list of reviews as input and return a list of predictions, where each prediction is either 0 (negative) or 1 (positive). Use the Gaussian Naive Bayes implementation from the scikit-learn library.

**Input:**
- A list of training reviews `X_train` (strings).
- A list of training labels `y_train` (integers, 0 or 1).
- A list of new reviews `new_reviews` (strings).

**Output:**
- A list of predicted labels (integers, 0 or 1) for each new review.

**Sample Input:**
```python
X_train = ["I hate this product", "This is the worst I have ever bought", 
           "Absolutely love this!", "I'm very happy with my purchase"]
y_train = [0, 0, 1, 1]
new_reviews = ["I love this product", "Not good, very disappointing"]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
[1, 0]
```

```python
from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import CountVectorizer
from sklearn.naive_bayes import GaussianNB
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
import numpy as np

def train_naive_bayes_classifier(X_train, y_train):
    """
    Trains a Naive Bayes classifier on the training data and returns the trained model.
    
    Args:
    X_train (list of str): List of training reviews.
    y_train (list of int): List of labels (0 for negative, 1 for positive).
    
    Returns:
    sklearn.pipeline.Pipeline: A pipeline object that includes CountVectorizer and GaussianNB.
    """
    pipeline = make_pipeline(CountVectorizer(), GaussianNB())
    pipeline.fit(X_train, y_train)
    return pipeline

def predict_review_sentiment(trained_model, new_reviews):
    """
    Classifies new reviews as either positive or negative.
    
    Args:
    trained_model (sklearn.pipeline.Pipeline): A trained model created by the train_naive_bayes_classifier function.
    new_reviews (list of str): List of reviews to be classified.
    
    Returns:
    list of int: List of predicted labels (0 for negative, 1 for positive) for each review.
    """
    predictions = trained_model.predict(new_reviews)
    return predictions

# Example usage
if __name__ == "__main__":
    X_train = ["I hate this product", "This is the worst I have ever bought", 
               "Absolutely love this!", "I'm very happy with my purchase"]
    y_train = [0, 0, 1, 1]
    new_reviews = ["I love this product", "Not good, very disappointing"]
    
    model = train_naive_bayes_classifier(X_train, y_train)
    predictions = predict_review_sentiment(model, new_reviews)
    
    print(predictions)

 Kuwait,general,"['<#sep#># Primitive Unit Cells (including Wigner–Seitz and voronoi cells)

If you’re googling “primitive cell,” I imagine you are a somewhat advanced student in materials science (or an extremely advanced PhD student in chemistry or physics?).

The primitive cell, or primitive lattice, is the smallest way to define a crystal cell or lattice.

Before you can understand primitive cells, first you need to understand the conventional cells. If you don’t know the “regular” crystal cells like FCC or BCC, I recommend you start with this article.

If you’re still here, I’ll assume you know some basics of crystals. For example, you know that the BCC unit cell has 2 atoms per cell in the traditional way of drawing the cell. It’s possible, however, to redraw the BCC crystal structure with only 1 atom per cell.

This redrawn perspective is called the primitive cell. The concept is more theoretical in nature and is more difficult for most people to grasp because it hides some of the symmetry that is shown in the traditional way of drawing the BCC cell.

In this article, I’ll explain why the primitive cell is useful, list them for common conventional unit cells, and also show you the Wigner-Sietz way to construct any unit cell.

### What are Crystals and the Unit Cell

Since this topic is geared towards more-advanced students, I’ll hide a refresher about crystals in collapsable text. If you want a full introduction to crystallography, you may want to read this article.

As you know, a crystal is made of a lattice + basis. The basis is the thing which is repeated (atoms, in our case), and the lattice is the pattern in which it is repeated. Here’s a 1-dimensional crystal.

The conventional way of identifying the unit cell is to draw it so that atoms are split evenly among different crystals. For example, in the image below, both the red box and blue box are valid depictions of the unit cell (in fact, any box of the same size would be a valid description).

However, the box I’ve drawn in red is more conventional, because it helps us remember that the crystal should repeat.

Take a look at this second example, with atoms spaced farther apart. In this case, if you drew the lattice to exactly encapsulate one atom (shown in blue), it would not make a unit cell. Draw another box to the right, and you’ll see that the two are not the same.

However, if you draw the box so that the left edge of the box contains half of one atom, and the right edge of the box contains half of a different atom, the spacing is always correct. The starting point does not matter, so you could redefine the unit cell as a rectangle with an atom in the center and empty space on both sides, but we usually leave it as I show with the red box.

There are many (actually, infinite) ways to draw the same crystal structure, but materials scientists usually use a certain convention. We typically draw the unit cell as the smallest cell that shows full symmetry, basing this structure on its underlying Bravais lattice.

Another, less common way to draw the unit cell is to draw it as small as possible, hiding symmetry relationships if necessary. Drawing the unit cell like this is called the primitive cell.

### Definition of the Primitive Cell

Let’s start with a 2-dimensional example of a conventional cell vs its primitive cell.

This is a centered rectangular Bravais lattice. Since the basis is simply one atom, we can also say that it is a centered rectangular crystal. There is ¼ of an atom on each corner of the rectangle, and 1 full atom in the center.

This gives us 2 atoms per unit cell, and each unit cell has a volume of .

This way of looking at the unit cell tells you the full symmetry. Atoms are closer together in the vertical direction compared to the horizontal direction, and there is a centering operation as well. In a real material, this kind of information about atoms can provide all sorts of clues about the material properties.

Now, look at the exact same crystal structure. None of the atoms have moved, but we are visualizing it differently. (Spoiler, this is the primitive cell). Instead of a rectangle, imagine that we have a rhombus. The sides are the same length, but the angle is not 90º.

Now, we have 1 atom per unit cell (it’s not exactly ¼ of an atom on each of the 4 corners, but it still adds up to 1 atom in total). The number of atoms per unit area hasn’t changed since we have the exact same crystal, so the area of this new unit cell must be smaller than the old one.

Specifically, the conventional unit cell had 2 atoms per cell, and the primitive one has 1 atom per cell. Therefore, the primitive unit cell has half the area of the conventional one.

Since the new unit cell has only 1 atom per cell, it is the smallest possible cell. Therefore, it is the primitive cell

Does that mean all primitive cells must have a single atom inside? Not necessarily.

Remember, there is a difference between “lattice point” and “atom.”

For every Bravais lattice, there exists a primitive lattice with a single lattice point in each cell. However, in a real crystal, each “lattice point” may be composed of multiple atoms.

For example, one of the most-common crystal structures is hexagonal close-packed (HCP). The HCP crystal is similar to the hexagonal Bravais lattice, except the HCP crystal has two atoms per lattice point.

The hexagonal prism can be sliced into parallelepipeds. If you are talking about the Bravais lattices, the conventional hexagonal lattice has 3 lattice points. If you looked at the primitive hexagonal lattice, there is only 1 lattice point.

If you are talking about the HCP crystal structure, the conventional HCP unit cell has 6 atoms, while the primitive HCP unit cell has 2 atoms.

I hope that illustrates how a primitive crystal cell can have more than one atom per unit cell, but a primitive Bravais lattice must have only 1 lattice point.

In fact, you have probably even seen an HCP primitive cell before.

### Primitive Versions of Conventional Unit Cells and Bravais Lattices (List)

You just saw the primitive cell for the HCP crystals, and the primitive lattice for the hexagonal lattice, but I’ll repeat them for the folks that skipped straight to this section.

Primitive Hexagonal Close-Packed

The primitive hexagonal lattice is the easiest to see, in my opinion. Take a vertical slice through ⅓ of the conventional cell, and there you have it! The hexagonal primitive lattice has 1 lattice point spread through the 8 corners of the parallelpiped.

The crystal structure corresponding to the hexagonal Bravais lattice, the Simple Hexagonal crystal structure, does occur in nature (for example, graphite). However, the Hexagonal Close-Packed (HCP) crystal is much more common.

The HCP primitive cell is a parallelpiped with 2 atoms inside. One atom is spread through the 8 corners, and the other atom rests in the center.

The primitive hexagonal cell can be described by the vectors:

And the basis:

Primitive Cubic

The Simple Cubic (SC) crystal structure is already a primitive cell. Yes, in many cases, the conventional cell is the primitive cell. Usually, the reason why we use a conventional cell is that the primitive cell doesn’t show full symmetry. In this case, the primitive SC cell does show full symmetry, so it is also the conventional cell. In fact, another name for Simple Cubic is “Primitive Cubic.”

Primitive cell of Simple Cubic (SC) Bravais Lattice can be described by vectors:

The volume of this cell is

Primitive Face-Centered Cubic (FCC)

The Face-Centered Cubic (FCC) crystal structure is the crystal structure with 1 atom at each lattice point in the FCC Bravais lattice.

To find the primitive cell, start with an atom as one corner of your cell, then find the 7 closest atoms which will make up the other corners. In theory, you could choose any random shape as long as it completely tiles 3D space and has a volume such that there is one atom per cell (there are infinite possibilities for primitive cells), but the easiest FCC primitive cell is shown below.

Primitive cell of Face-Centered Cubic (FCC) Bravais Lattice can be described by vectors:

The volume of this cell is

This cell has 3 side lengths equal to each other and the three vectors have 120º angles to each other.

The volume of the primitive cell is , or ¼ the volume of the conventional FCC unit cell. You can find this number using geometry calculations, or by using the knowledge that the primitive cell has the same atoms/volume as the conventional cell, and since the conventional cell has 4 times as many atoms per cell, the primitive cell must have ¼ the volume.

Primitive Body-Centered Cubic (BCC)

To find the BCC primitive cell, you can use the<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Bookshelf` class to manage a collection of books. Each book is characterized by its title, author, and genre. Your class must support the following operations:

1. **Add a Book**: Given a title, author, and genre, add the book to the collection.
2. **Remove a Book**: Given a title, remove the book from the collection if it exists.
3. **List Books by Genre**: Given a genre, return a list of titles of books that belong to that genre. If no books belong to the specified genre, return an empty list.

The book data should be stored in a dictionary where the keys are the titles of the books, and the values are dictionaries containing the author and genre of the book.

Implement the `Bookshelf` class with the following methods:
- `add_book(title, author, genre)`: Adds a book to the collection.
- `remove_book(title)`: Removes a book from the collection if it exists.
- `list_books_by_genre(genre)`: Returns a list of titles of books that belong to the specified genre.

**Sample Input/Output**:
```python
bookshelf = Bookshelf()
bookshelf.add_book("1984", "George Orwell", "Dystopian")
bookshelf.add_book("Brave New World", "Aldous Huxley", "Dystopian")
bookshelf.add_book("To Kill a Mockingbird", "Harper Lee", "Fiction")
print(bookshelf.list_books_by_genre("Dystopian"))  # Output: ['1984', 'Brave New World']
bookshelf.remove_book("1984")
print(bookshelf.list_books_by_genre("Dystopian"))  # Output: ['Brave New World']
print(bookshelf.list_books_by_genre("Fiction"))    # Output: ['To Kill a Mockingbird']
print(bookshelf.list_books_by_genre("Science"))    # Output: []
```

```python
class Bookshelf:
    def __init__(self):
        self.books = {}

    def add_book(self, title, author, genre):
        """
        Adds a book to the collection.

        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param genre: The genre of the book.
        """
        self.books[title] = {'author': author, 'genre': genre}

    def remove_book(self, title):
        """
        Removes a book from the collection if it exists.

        :param title: The title of the book to be removed.
        """
        if title in self.books:
            del self.books[title]

    def list_books_by_genre(self, genre):
        """
        Returns a list of titles of books that belong to the specified genre.

        :param genre: The genre of the books to be listed.
        :return: A list of titles of books that belong to the specified genre.
        """
        return [title for title, info in self.books.items() if info['genre'] == genre]

### Wigner-Seitz and Voronoi

So far we have constructed primitive cells using a mixture of intuition and guess-and-check (or you just peeked at the answer).

The primitive cells I’ve shown you so far are the standard primitive cells–they don’t have the full symmetry of the non-primitive conventional cells, but they are built to be similar to conventional cells.

There is another method of using math to generate a different set of primitive cells (remember, each primitive cell is one of infinite ways to describe the same pattern).

The primitive cells created by this method are called Wigner-Seitz unit cells.

Rather than splitting the atom to put ⅛ of it on each of the 8 corners, the Wigner-Seitz unit cell has a single atom surrounded by empty space.

Wigner-Seitz cells are unique, but they are not that intuitive to understand as a crystal structure. However, the algorithm to create these cells is simple. Let’s start with an example in 2D.

1. Choose any lattice point to start. This atom will be the only atom to appear in the cell, and it will be exactly in the center.
1. Draw a line from your center atom to every other atom. You’ll see that most of these will be redundant, so in practice you only need to draw lines to the close atoms. We’ll color these lines red.
1. Find the midpoint of each red line.
1. From the midpoint, draw a blue line perpendicular to the red line. The blue lines should be infinitely long.
1. The perpendicular blue lines will form the outline of a polygon. The smallest polygon formed is the primitive cell.

If you’ve done this correctly, your polygon should completely fill the space!

What I have described is an algorithm for creating Voronai polygons. Voronai polygons are a mathematical concept which describes the set of all space which is closer to one point than any other.

The Wigner-Seitz unit cell is the unit cell which is generated from the voronai technique. There are also Voronai polyhedra, which are the 3-dimensional equivalent.

When making 3D Wigner-Seitz cells, use the same procedure that I outlined. However, instead of drawing blue lines perpendicular to the red lines, draw blue planes perpendicular to the red lines.

The planes which intersect to form the smallest polyhedron is your Wigner-Seitz cell!

However, a note of caution: the method of generating Wigner-Seitz primitive cells only works when you center the cell around a lattice point

### Final Thoughts

Primitive cells or primitive lattices are the best way to describe atomic crystal structures with math. Primitive cells are the smallest possible cell. There are an infinite number of primitive cells, but materials scientists typically use one of two types: the conventional primitive lattice has ⅛ of a  lattice point at each corner of a parallelepiped, and the Wigner-Seitz primitive cell has a lattice point at the center of each polyhedron.<#sep#>You are given a string `text` and a list of keywords `keywords`. Your task is to implement a function `highlight_keywords(text, keywords)` that returns a modified version of `text` with all occurrences of the `keywords` highlighted. Highlighting is achieved by wrapping the keyword with `<strong>` and `</strong>` tags. The function should be case-insensitive and highlight all occurrences of the keywords.

**Input:**
- A string `text` (1 ≤ |text| ≤ 1000)
- A list of keywords `keywords` (1 ≤ |keywords| ≤ 100), where each keyword is a non-empty string

**Output:**
- A string with all occurrences of the keywords highlighted

**Sample Input:**
```
text = "The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog"
keywords = ["quick", "lazy"]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
"The <strong>quick</strong> brown fox jumps over the <strong>lazy</strong> dog"
```

```python
import re

def highlight_keywords(text, keywords):
    """
    Highlights all occurrences of the given keywords in the text.
    
    Parameters:
    text (str): The input text.
    keywords (list of str): The list of keywords to highlight.
    
    Returns:
    str: The text with all occurrences of the keywords highlighted.
    """
    # Convert the text to lowercase for case-insensitive matching
    lower_text = text.lower()
    # Create a set of lowercase keywords for faster lookup
    lower_keywords = set(keyword.lower() for keyword in keywords)
    
    # Create a list to store the highlighted parts
    highlighted_parts = []
    last_end = 0
    
    # Iterate over the text to find all occurrences of the keywords
    for match in sorted(re.finditer(r'\b\w+\b', lower_text), key=lambda m: m.start()):
        word = match.group()
        start, end = match.span()
        # If the word is a keyword, highlight it
        if word in lower_keywords:
            # Append the text before the keyword (if any)
            highlighted_parts.append(text[last_end:start])
            # Append the highlighted keyword
            highlighted_parts.append(f"<strong>{text[start:end]}</strong>")
            last_end = end
        # If the word is not a keyword, move on
        else:
            last_end = end
    
    # Append the remaining text after the last keyword (if any)
    highlighted_parts.append(text[last_end:])
    
    # Join all parts to form the final highlighted text
    highlighted_text = ''.join(highlighted_parts)
    return highlighted_text

# Sample usage
text = "The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog"
keywords = ["quick", "lazy"]
highlighted_text = highlight_keywords(text, keywords)
print(highlighted_text)

- `__init__(self, title, author, isbn, publication_year)`: Initializes a new book with the given attributes and sets `available` to `True`.

**Sample Input:**
```
book1 = Book("1984", "George Orwell", "1234567890", 1949)
print(book1)

'1984' by George Orwell, ISBN: 1234567890, Published: 1949, Available: True

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, isbn, publication_year):
        """
        Initializes a new book with the given attributes and sets available to True.
        
        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param isbn: The ISBN of the book.
        :param publication_year: The publication year of the book.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.isbn = isbn
        self.publication_year = publication_year
        self.available = True

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the book.
        
        :return: A string describing the book.
        """
        return f"'{self.title}' by {self.author}, ISBN: {self.isbn}, Published: {self.publication_year}, Available: {self.available}"

    def checkout(self):
        """
        Marks the book as checked out if it is available.
        Prints an error message if the book is already checked out.
        """
        if self.available:
            self.available = False
            print(f"Book '{self.title}' has been checked out.")
        else:
            print(f"Book '{self.title}' is already checked out.")

    def return_book(self):
        """
        Marks the book as returned if it is not available.
        Prints an error message if the book was not checked out.
        """
        if not self.available:
            self.available = True
            print(f"Book '{self.title}' has been returned.")
        else:
            print(f"Book '{self.title}' was not checked out.")

Your task is to implement a function `find_kth_smallest` that returns the k-th smallest element in the list. The function should have a time complexity better than O(n log n).

**Constraints:**
- 1 ≤ k ≤ len(nums)
- 1 ≤ len(nums) ≤ 10^5
- -10^9 ≤ nums[i] ≤ 10^9

**Sample Input:**
```
nums = [3, 2, 1, 5, 6, 4]
k = 2
```

**Sample Output:**
```
2
```

```python
import random

def partition(nums, low, high):
    """
    Partitions the list around a pivot element such that elements less than the pivot
    are on the left, and elements greater than the pivot are on the right.
    
    :param nums: List of integers to be partitioned.
    :param low: Starting index of the partition range.
    :param high: Ending index of the partition range.
    :return: The final position of the pivot element.
    """
    pivot_index = random.randint(low, high)
    pivot_value = nums[pivot_index]
    nums[pivot_index], nums[high] = nums[high], nums[pivot_index]
    store_index = low
    for i in range(low, high):
        if nums[i] < pivot_value:
            nums[i], nums[store_index] = nums[store_index], nums[i]
            store_index += 1
    nums[store_index], nums[high] = nums[high], nums[store_index]
    return store_index

def quickselect(nums, low, high, k_smallest):
    """
    Selects the k-th smallest element in the list using the Quickselect algorithm.
    
    :param nums: List of integers from which to select the k-th smallest element.
    :param low: Starting index of the selection range.
    :param high: Ending index of the selection range.
    :param k_smallest: The order of the smallest element to find (0-based index).
    :return: The k-th smallest element.
    """
    if low == high:
        return nums[low]
    
    pivot_index = partition(nums, low, high)
    
    if k_smallest == pivot_index:
        return nums[k_smallest]
    elif k_smallest < pivot_index:
        return quickselect(nums, low, pivot_index - 1, k_smallest)
    else:
        return quickselect(nums, pivot_index + 1, high, k_smallest)

def find_kth_smallest(nums, k):
    """
    Finds the k-th smallest element in the list.
    
    :param nums: List of integers.
    :param k: The order of the smallest element to find (1-based index).
    :return: The k-th smallest element.
    :raises ValueError: If k is out of bounds.
    """
    if not 1 <= k <= len(nums):
        raise ValueError("k is out of bounds!")
    return quickselect(nums, 0, len(nums) - 1, k - 1)

# Example usage:
nums = [3, 2, 1, 5, 6, 4]
k = 2
print(find_kth_smallest(nums, k))  # Output: 2

`Book` class in Python. The class should have the following attributes: `title`, `author`, `year_published`, and `isbn`. Implement methods to set each of these attributes and a method to retrieve a formatted string containing all the book's information. The `isbn` attribute must be validated to ensure it consists of exactly 13 digits. Your implementation should include a comprehensive docstring for the class and its methods.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
book = Book()
book.set_title("1984")
book.set_author("George Orwell")
book.set_year_published(1949)
book.set_isbn("9780451524935")
print(book.get_info())
```

**Expected Output:**
```
Title: 1984
Author: George Orwell
Year Published: 1949
ISBN: 9780451524935
```

```python
class Book:
    """
    A class to represent a Book.

    Attributes:
    ----------
    title : str
        the title of the book
    author : str
        the author of the book
    year_published : int
        the year the book was published
    isbn : str
        the International Standard Book Number of the book

    Methods
    -------
    set_title(title: str)
        Sets the title of the book.

    set_author(author: str)
        Sets the author of the book.

    set_year_published(year_published: int)
        Sets the year the book was published.

    set_isbn(isbn: str)
        Sets the ISBN of the book, validates that it has 13 digits.

    get_info() -> str
        Returns a string containing the book's title, author, year published, and ISBN.
    """

    def __init__(self, title='', author='', year_published=0, isbn=''):
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.year_published = year_published
        self.isbn = isbn

    def set_title(self, title: str):
        """
        Sets the title of the book.

        Parameters:
        ----------
        title : str
            The title of the book.
        """
        self.title = title

    def set_author(self, author: str):
        """
        Sets the author of the book.

        Parameters:
        ----------
        author : str
            The author of the book.
        """
        self.author = author

    def set_year_published(self, year_published: int):
        """
        Sets the year the book was published.

        Parameters:
        ----------
        year_published : int
            The year the book was published.
        """
        self.year_published = year_published

    def set_isbn(self, isbn: str):
        """
        Sets the ISBN of the book, validates that it has 13 digits.

        Parameters:
        ----------
        isbn : str
            The ISBN of the book.

        Raises:
        ------
        ValueError
            If the ISBN is not a 13-digit string.
        """
        if isinstance(isbn, str) and len(isbn) == 13 and isbn.isdigit():
            self.isbn = isbn
        else:
            raise ValueError("ISBN must be a 13-digit string.")

    def get_info(self) -> str:
        """
        Returns a string containing the book's title, author, year published, and ISBN.

        Returns:
        -------
        str
            A formatted string with the book's information.
        """
        return (f"Title: {self.title}\n"
                f"Author: {self.author}\n"
                f"Year Published: {self.year_published}\n"
                f"ISBN: {self.isbn}")
```<#sep#> a unique answer. Therefore, the answer must be the same regardless of the coloring, as long as the edge constraints are satisfied. implementing a simple bank account simulator. The simulator should support the following operations:
- **Deposit**: Add a specified amount to the account balance.
- **Withdraw**: Subtract a specified amount from the account balance, provided there are sufficient funds.
- **Check Balance**: Display the current account balance.

The program should interact with the user through a text-based interface. The user will input commands to perform these operations. The program should handle invalid inputs gracefully.

**Input:**
- The first line contains an integer `n` (1 ≤ n ≤ 100), the number of operations to perform.
- The next `n` lines each contain an operation in the format:
  - `deposit X` where `X` is a positive number representing the amount to deposit.
  - `withdraw X` where `X` is a positive number representing the amount to withdraw.
  - `check_balance` to display the current balance.

**Output:**
- For each `deposit` and `withdraw` operation, print the updated balance after the operation.
- For each `check_balance` operation, print the current balance.
- If an invalid operation is attempted (e.g., negative deposit/withdraw amount, insufficient funds), print an appropriate error message.

**Sample Input:**
```
5
deposit 100
withdraw 50
check_balance
withdraw 60
check_balance
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Deposited $100.00, new balance is $100.00.
Withdrew $50.00, new balance is $50.00.
Current balance is $50.00.
Insufficient funds.
Current balance is $50.00.
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, initial_balance=0):
        self.balance = initial_balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount into the account.
        :param amount: The amount to deposit (must be positive).
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
            print(f"Deposited ${amount:.2f}, new balance is ${self.balance:.2f}.")
        else:
            print("Deposit amount must be positive.")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount from the account.
        :param amount: The amount to withdraw (must be positive and less than or equal to the current balance).
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if self.balance >= amount:
                self.balance -= amount
                print(f"Withdrew ${amount:.2f}, new balance is ${self.balance:.2f}.")
            else:
                print("Insufficient funds.")
        else:
            print("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")

    def check_balance(self):
        """
        Check and print the current balance of the account.
        """
        print(f"Current balance is ${self.balance:.2f}.")

def main():
    account = BankAccount(0)  # Start with a balance of 0
    n = int(input().strip())
    for _ in range(n):
        command = input().strip().split()
        if command[0] == 'deposit':
            try:
                amount = float(command[1])
                account.deposit(amount)
            except ValueError:
                print("Invalid input. Please enter a number.")
        elif command[0] == 'withdraw':
            try:
                amount = float(command[1])
                account.withdraw(amount)
            except ValueError:
                print("Invalid input. Please enter a number.")
        elif command[0] == 'check_balance':
            account.check_balance()
        else:
            print("Invalid command.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

with implementing a simple bank account system. Your system should support the following operations:

1. **Deposit**: Add a specified amount to the account balance. The deposit amount must be positive.
2. **Withdraw**: Subtract a specified amount from the account balance, provided the balance does not go below zero. The withdrawal amount must be positive.
3. **Display Balance**: Print the current balance of the account formatted to two decimal places.

Implement a Python class `BankAccount` that supports these operations. The class should be initialized with an optional `initial_balance` parameter, which defaults to 0.0 if not provided.

**Sample Input/Output**:
```
account = BankAccount(100.0)
account.deposit(50.0)
account.display_balance()  # Output: Current balance: $150.00
account.withdraw(30.0)
account.display_balance()  # Output: Current balance: $120.00
account.withdraw(150.0)    # Raises ValueError: Insufficient funds
account.deposit(-10.0)     # Raises ValueError: Deposit amount must be positive
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    """
    A simple class to represent a bank account.

    Attributes
    ----------
    balance : float
        The current balance of the account.

    Methods
    -------
    deposit(amount: float) -> None:
        Adds the given amount to the current balance.

    withdraw(amount: float) -> None:
        Subtracts the given amount from the current balance, 
        provided the balance does not go below zero.

    display_balance() -> None:
        Prints the current balance.
    """

    def __init__(self, initial_balance: float = 0.0):
        """
        Constructs all the necessary attributes for the bank account object.

        Parameters
        ----------
        initial_balance : float, optional
            The initial balance of the account (default is 0.0).
        """
        self.balance = initial_balance

    def deposit(self, amount: float) -> None:
        """
        Adds the given amount to the current balance.

        Parameters
        ----------
        amount : float
            The amount to be deposited into the account.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
        else:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive")

    def withdraw(self, amount: float) -> None:
        """
        Subtracts the given amount from the current balance, 
        provided the balance does not go below zero.

        Parameters
        ----------
        amount : float
            The amount to be withdrawn from the account.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if self.balance >= amount:
                self.balance -= amount
            else:
                raise ValueError("Insufficient funds")
        else:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive")

    def display_balance(self) -> None:
        """
        Prints the current balance.
        """
        print(f"Current balance: ${self.balance:.2f}")

```python
def f(a,b):
    if a==0:return b+1
    if b==0:return f(a-1,1)
    return f(a-1,f(a,b-1))
print(f(1000,3))
```

### Explanation:
This program implements a variant of the fast-growing hierarchy in Python. The function `f(a, b)` corresponds to \( f_{\alpha}(b) \) where \(\alpha\) is derived from parameter `a`. The recursion depth here allows us to reach an ordinal significantly higher than \(\theta(\Omega_\omega \omega)\), ensuring the output surpasses TREE(3).

1. **Function Definition**: 
   - `f(a, b)` is defined recursively.
   - When `a==0`, it returns \( b+1 \), corresponding to \( f_0(b) \).
   - When `b==0`, it recurses to the previous level (i.e., \( f_{\alpha-1}(1) \)).
   - Otherwise, it continues nesting deeper into the hierarchy, which effectively computes \( f_{\alpha}(b) \) based on the fast-growing hierarchy rules.

2. **Ordinal Representation**:
   - The parameter `a` acts as a proxy for the ordinal. Increasing `a` increases the level in the fast-growing hierarchy exponentially. For example:
     - `f(1, 3)` corresponds to \( f_{\omega}(3) \)
     - `f(2, 3)` corresponds to \( f_{\omega^\omega}(3) \)
     - `f(1000, 3)` reaches an ordinal like \(\omega \uparrow\uparrow 1000\), which is far beyond the ordinal for TREE(3).

3. **Output Justification**:
   - The function with `a=1000` evaluates to a function beyond \( f_{\theta(\Omega_\omega \omega + 1)}(3) \). Given that TREE(3) is approximated by \( f_{\theta(\Omega_\omega \omega)}(3) \), this ensures the output is sufficiently large.

### Ungolfed Version:
```python
def fast_growing_hierarchy(alpha, beta):
    """
    Implements the fast-growing hierarchy function f_alpha(beta)
    """
    if alpha == 0:
        return beta + 1
    if beta == 0:
        return fast_growing_hierarchy(alpha - 1, 1)
    return fast_growing_hierarchy(alpha - 1, fast_growing_hierarchy(alpha, beta - 1))

# Calculate a number much larger than TREE(3)
result = fast_growing_hierarchy(1000, 3)

Python that simulates basic banking operations. The class should support depositing money, withdrawing money, and checking the current balance. The constructor should allow initializing the account with an optional starting balance. Implement error handling for invalid operations, such as attempting to withdraw more than the current balance or depositing a negative amount. Your implementation should include docstrings for the class and its methods.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- **Input:** 
  ```python
  account = BankAccount(100.0)
  account.deposit(50.0)
  account.withdraw(30.0)
  print(account.display_balance())
  ```
- **Output:**
  ```
  120.0
  ```

```python
class BankAccount:
    """
    A simple representation of a bank account.

    Attributes:
    ----------
    balance : float
        The current balance of the account.

    Methods
    -------
    deposit(amount):
        Deposits a specified amount into the account.
    withdraw(amount):
        Withdraws a specified amount from the account, if sufficient funds are available.
    display_balance():
        Returns the current balance of the account.
    """

    def __init__(self, starting_balance=0.0):
        """
        Initializes the bank account with an optional starting balance.

        Parameters:
        ----------
        starting_balance : float, optional
            The starting balance of the account (default is 0.0).
        """
        self.balance = starting_balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposits a specified amount into the account.

        Parameters:
        ----------
        amount : float
            The amount to deposit into the account.

        Raises:
        ------
        ValueError
            If the amount to deposit is negative.
        """
        if amount < 0:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive.")
        self.balance += amount

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraws a specified amount from the account, if sufficient funds are available.

        Parameters:
        ----------
        amount : float
            The amount to withdraw from the account.

        Raises:
        ------
        ValueError
            If the amount to withdraw is negative or greater than the current balance.
        """
        if amount < 0:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")
        if amount > self.balance:
            raise ValueError("Insufficient funds for withdrawal.")
        self.balance -= amount

    def display_balance(self):
        """
        Returns the current balance of the account.

        Returns:
        -------
        float
            The current balance of the account.
        """
        return self.balance

### Ungolfed Version:
```python
def fast_growing_hierarchy(alpha, beta):
    """
    Implements the fast-growing hierarchy function f_alpha(beta)
    """
    if alpha == 0:
        return beta + 1
    if beta == 0:
        return fast_growing_hierarchy(alpha - 1, 1)
    return fast_growing_hierarchy(alpha - 1, fast_growing_hierarchy(alpha, beta - 1))

# Calculate a number much larger than TREE(3)
result = fast_growing_hierarchy(1000, 3)
print(result)
```

The golfed version minimizes the syntax while preserving the recursive structure necessary to reach the required ordinal level. The `1000` in the call ensures we far exceed the ordinal needed for TREE(3).<#sep#>', 'PO', 'YB', 'YC', 'YD', 'YE', 'YF', 'YG', 'YH', '7A', '7B', '7C', '7D', '7E', '7F', '7G', '7H', '7I', '8A', '8B', '8C', '8D', '8E', '8F', '8G', '8H', '8I']",['AAA-ZZZ'],"^(JZ|PK|PL|PM|PN|PO|YB|YC|YD|YE|YF|YG|YH|7A|7B|7C|7D|7E|7F|7G|7H|7I|8A|8B|8C|8D|8E|8F|8G|8H|8I)(-{0,1}(([A-Z][A-Z][A-Z])|([A-Z0-9]{1,4}))){0,1}$"

Python class `FileAnalyzer` that processes a text file to perform several analyses. Your class should be able to handle the following tasks:

1. Count the total number of lines in the file.
2. Count the total number of words in the file.
3. Count the total number of characters in the file.
4. Identify and return the most frequently occurring word in the file.
5. Identify and return the least frequently occurring word in the file.

The class should have the following methods:

- `__init__(self, filename)`: Initializes the class with the filename of the text file to be analyzed.
- `count_lines(self)`: Returns the total number of lines in the file.
- `count_words(self)`: Returns the total number of words in the file.
- `count_characters(self)`: Returns the total number of characters in the file.
- `most_frequent_word(self)`: Returns the most frequently occurring word in the file.
- `least_frequent_word(self)`: Returns the least frequently occurring word in the file.

**Sample Input File (sample.txt):**
```
Hello world
Hello everyone
This is a test
```

**Sample Output:**
- `count_lines()`: 3
- `count_words()`: 8
- `count_characters()`: 44
- `most_frequent_word()`: "hello"
- `least_frequent_word()`: "everyone", "is", "a", "test" (any one of these)

```python
import re
from collections import Counter

class FileAnalyzer:
    def __init__(self, filename):
        """
        Initializes the FileAnalyzer with the given filename.
        
        :param filename: str, the path to the text file to be analyzed.
        """
        self.filename = filename
        self.content = None
        self.words = None
        self.read_file()

    def read_file(self):
        """
        Reads the content of the file and extracts words.
        """
        with open(self.filename, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as file:
            self.content = file.read()
            self.words = re.findall(r'\b\w+\b', self.content.lower())

    def count_lines(self):
        """
        Counts the total number of lines in the file.
        
        :return: int, the number of lines.
        """
        return len(self.content.splitlines())

    def count_words(self):
        """
        Counts the total number of words in the file.
        
        :return: int, the number of words.
        """
        return len(self.words)

    def count_characters(self):
        """
        Counts the total number of characters in the file.
        
        :return: int, the number of characters.
        """
        return len(self.content)

    def most_frequent_word(self):
        """
        Identifies and returns the most frequently occurring word in the file.
        
        :return: str, the most frequent word.
        """
        if not self.words:
            return None
        word_counts = Counter(self.words)
        most_common_word, _ = word_counts.most_common(1)[0]
        return most_common_word

    def least_frequent_word(self):
        """
        Identifies and returns the least frequently occurring word in the file.
        
        :return: str, the least frequent word.
        """
        if not self.words:
            return None
        word_counts = Counter(self.words)
        least_common_word, _ = word_counts.most_common()[-1]
        return least_common_word

Specification:
Language: ECMAScript, any version[1]
Challenge: Somewhat like python's itertools.groupBy: Your function Array.group(f) should take as input an "equivalence function" f whose output defines our notion of an "equivalence key" for array elements (like a sort key). The return value of .group(f) should be an array of arrays in the same order, split whenever the value of the keyfunc parameter changes (in the === sense).
Object.defineProperties(Array.prototype, {
    group: {
        value: function(f) {/*
            CODE GOES HERE
        */}
    }
});

Examples / test cases:
> ['a','c','E','G','n'].group(x => x==x.toUpperCase())
[['a','c'], ['E','G'], ['n']]

> [1,1,'1'].group(x=>x)
[[1,1],['1']]

> [3,1,'1',10,5,5].group(x=>x+2)
[[3], [1], ['1'], [10], [5,5]]

> [{a:1,b:2,c:3}, {b:2,a:1,c:0}, {c:5}].group(x => JSON.stringify([x.a, x.b]))
[[{a:1,b:2,c:3},{b:2,a:1,c:0}], [{c:5}]]

> [].group(x=>x)
[]

Scoring criteria:
score = # of characters that replace comment above (lower is better), with easements that:

whitespace and statement-separating semicolons don't count towards total[2]
variable names and keywords each count as exactly 1 characters towards total per use[3]; this includes builtins, so Array.map.apply would be 5 'characters' (however, builtins count their full length as tiebreakers)
-5 bonus points if f being undefined has same result as the identity function x=>x
you may modify the construct function(f) {...} for recursion/combinator purposes while keeping it as a function respecting rule #5, but must subtract from your score the extra characters incurred (i.e. imagine the scoring began earlier)

Disallowances:

no side-effects which affect anything other than your code (such as modifying this, window, the prototype, etc.); as a corollary, all definitions must stay local to the .group function (e.g. x=5 would be disallowed, but var x=5 is fine)

minor notes:
[1] draft specs fine if in any major browser, proprietary extensions fine if in 2+ major browsers;
[2] as long as you don't try to compile a few megabytes-long whitespace ;-)
[3] as long as reflection is not used to abuse code-in-variable-names ;-)

Errata:
Answer may do anything reasonable with regards to sparse arrays. e.g. arr=[1,1]; arr[4]=1; arr -> [1, 1, empty × 2, 1]. Possibilities include ignoring them (like .map and friends do), or treating them as undefined (like an incremental for-loop would), or anything reasonable. For example arr.group(x=>x) might return [[1,1],[undefined,undefined],[1]], or [[1,1,1]], or even more dubious things like [[1,1],null,[1]] if it makes sense. You may also assume sparse arrays do not exist.

diff --git a/graph_lib/directed_edge.py b/graph_lib/directed_edge.py

+from __future__ import annotations
+
+from graph_lib.edge import Edge
+
+
+class DirectedEdge(Edge):
+    """
+    The direction of the edge goes:
+
+    DirectedEdge.vertex_a -> DirectedEdge.vertex_b.
+    """
+
+    def __eq__(self, other: DirectedEdge) -> bool:
+        """
+        Compares two edges by the ids of the vertecies.
+
+        :param other: DirectedEdge to compare against
+        :return: Equality as boolean
+        """
+        if isinstance(other, self.__class__):
+            return (self.vertex_a == other.vertex_a
+                    and self.vertex_b == other.vertex_b)
+
+    def __str__(self) -> str:
+        return f'Edge Id {self.id}: ' \
+            f'{self.vertex_a.id} -> {self.vertex_b.id}\n'
diff --git a/graph_lib/directed_graph.py b/graph_lib/directed_graph.py
new file mode 100644
index 0000000..849332d
--- /dev/null
+++ b/graph_lib/directed_graph.py

+from __future__ import annotations
+from typing import List
+
+import os
+
+from graph_lib.graph import Graph
+from graph_lib.directed_edge import DirectedEdge
+from graph_lib.vertex import Vertex
+
+
+class DirectedGraph(Graph):
+
+    def __init__(self, verticies: List[Vertex], edges: List[DirectedEdge]):
+        """
+        Constructs a directed Graph.
+
+        :param vertecies: List of vertecies
+        :param edges: List of directed edges
+        """
+        super().__init__(verticies, edges)
+
+    @classmethod
+    def from_file(cls: DirectedGraph, file: str) -> Graph:
+        """
+        Constructs a directed graph from a file of the form:
+
+        <number of vertecies>
+        <vertecie a of edge 1> <vertecie b of edge 1>
+        ...
+
+        :param file: Path to the file (can either be a relative path from
+                     the current cwd or an absolute one).
+        :return: a directed Graph object.
+        """
+        if not os.path.isabs(file):
+            file = f'{os.getcwd()}/{file}'
+
+        vertecies: List = []
+        edges: List[DirectedEdge] = []
+
+        with open(file, 'r') as f:
+            for i, line in enumerate(f):
+                if i == 0:
+                    num_verticies = int(line)
+                    vertecies = [Vertex(x) for x in range(num_verticies)]
+                    continue
+                input_vertecies = line.split()
+                edges.append(DirectedEdge(vertecies[int(input_vertecies[0])],
+                                          vertecies[int(input_vertecies[1])],
+                                          i-1))
+        return cls(vertecies, edges)
+
+    def insert_edge(self, edge: DirectedEdge) -> None:
+        """
+        Inserts a edge to the graph.
+        If the vertecies do not already exist they are added to the graph.
+        Performs health checks, e.g. no same vertex/edge ids.
+
+        :param edge: The edge to add.
+        :return: None
+        """
+        for elem in self.edges:
+            if elem == edge:
+                raise ValueError(f'Edge id already exists in the graph.')
+
+        try:
+            self.insert_vertex(edge.vertex_a)
+        except ValueError:
+            pass
+
+        try:
+            self.insert_vertex(edge.vertex_b)
+        except ValueError:
+            pass
+
+        if edge.id > self._current_highest_edge_id:
+            self._current_highest_edge_id = edge.id
+        self.edges.append(edge)
+
+    def create_edge(self, vertex_a: Vertex, vertex_b: Vertex,
+                    edge_id: int = None) -> DirectedEdge:
+        """
+        Creates an edge and adds it to the graph.
+
+        :param vertex_a: Vertex
+        :param vertex_b: Vertex
+        :param edge_id: A edge id. If None one is automatically assigned.
+                        Defaults to None.
+        :return: Created Edge
+        """
+        if edge_id is None:
+            edge_id = self.get_free_edge_id()
+
+        new_edge = DirectedEdge(vertex_a, vertex_b, edge_id)
+        self.insert_edge(new_edge)
+        return new_edge
+
+    def get_edge_by_id(self, edge_id: int) -> DirectedEdge:
+        """
+        Gets a vertex from the graph by id.
+
+        :param edge_id: The id to be searched.
+        :return: The found vertex
+        """
+        for edge in self.edges:
+            if edge.id == edge_id:
+                return edge
+        raise ValueError(f'{edge_id} does not exist in {self.edges}')
+
+    def create_vertex(self, contained_edges: List[DirectedEdge] = None,
+                      vertex_id: int = None) -> Vertex:
+        """
+        Creates a vertex and inserts it into the graph.
+        If the vertex has edges assaigned to it they are
+        also added to the graph.
+
+        :param contained_edges: List of directed edges on the vertex.
+                                Note: They will be added to the graph.
+        :param vertex_id: A vertex id. If None one is automatically assigned.
+                          Defaults to None.
+        :return: Created Vertex
+        """
+        return super().create_vertex(contained_edges, vertex_id)
+
+    def __str__(self) -> str:
+        return f'Directed Graph with {self.num_verticies}' \
+            f' number of vertecies and edges {self.edges}'

3 7 3 3 7 1 6 5 7 4 4 8 6 8 5 3 4 5 5 2 4 5 7 6 5 3 5 4 4 3 5 3<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Book` class for a library management system. The class should encapsulate the properties of a book and provide methods to manage its availability. The `Book` class should have the following attributes:

- `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
- `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
- `isbn`: A string representing the ISBN number of the book.
- `year`: An integer representing the year the book was published.
- `available_copies`: An integer representing the number of copies currently available for checkout.
- `total_copies`: An integer representing the total number of copies in the library.

The class should also provide the following methods:

- `check_out`: Decrease the number of available copies by 1 if there are any available. If no copies are available, print a message stating that the book is currently not available.
- `return_book`: Increase the number of available copies by 1, ensuring it does not exceed the total number of copies.
- `get_details`: Return a string with the book's details in the format: "{title} by {author} (ISBN: {isbn}, Year: {year}) - {available_copies}/{total_copies} copies available".

Ensure that `available_copies` and `total_copies` are non-negative and that `available_copies` does not exceed `total_copies`.

**Sample Input/Output:**

```python
book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", "9780451524935", 1949, 3, 5)
print(book.get_details())  # Output: "1984 by George Orwell (ISBN: 9780451524935, Year: 1949) - 3/5 copies available"
book.check_out()
print(book.get_details())  # Output: "1984 by George Orwell (ISBN: 9780451524935, Year: 1949) - 2/5 copies available"
book.return_book()
print(book.get_details())  # Output: "1984 by George Orwell (ISBN: 9780451524935, Year: 1949) - 3/5 copies available"
book.check_out()
book.check_out()
book.check_out()
book.check_out()  # Output: "The book '1984' is currently not available."
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, isbn, year, available_copies, total_copies):
        """
        Initialize a new Book instance.

        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param isbn: The ISBN number of the book.
        :param year: The year the book was published.
        :param available_copies: The number of copies currently available.
        :param total_copies: The total number of copies in the library.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.isbn = isbn
        self.year = year
        self.available_copies = max(0, min(available_copies, total_copies))
        self.total_copies = max(0, total_copies)

    def check_out(self):
        """
        Decrease the number of available copies by 1 if available.
        Print a message if no copies are available.
        """
        if self.available_copies > 0:
            self.available_copies -= 1
        else:
            print(f"The book '{self.title}' is currently not available.")

    def return_book(self):
        """
        Increase the number of available copies by 1, ensuring it does not exceed total copies.
        """
        if self.available_copies < self.total_copies:
            self.available_copies += 1

    def get_details(self):
        """
        Return a string with the book's details.

        :return: A formatted string with the book's details.
        """
        return (f"{self.title} by {self.author} (ISBN: {self.isbn}, Year: {self.year})"
                f" - {self.available_copies}/{self.total_copies} copies available")

4 4 5 7 2 4 <#sep#>You are tasked with developing a program to generate random sentences using a Markov chain. Given a text file `corpus.txt` containing a large corpus of text, implement a function `generate_sentences(filename, sentence_count, sentence_length)` that reads the text file, constructs a Markov chain, and generates `sentence_count` random sentences, each of length `sentence_length`. Sentences are considered to be terminated by a period, exclamation mark, or question mark. Assume that the text file `corpus.txt` is in the same directory as your Python script.

**Input:**
- `filename`: A string representing the name of the text file.
- `sentence_count`: An integer representing the number of sentences to generate.
- `sentence_length`: An integer representing the length of each sentence.

**Output:**
- A string containing `sentence_count` sentences, each of length `sentence_length`, separated by newlines.

**Sample Input:**
`corpus.txt` contains:
```
Hello world! This is a test. Hello again!
```
`sentence_count = 2`
`sentence_length = 5`

**Sample Output:**
```
Hello world! This
is a test. Hello
```

```python
import random
import re
from collections import defaultdict

def generate_sentences(filename, sentence_count, sentence_length):
    """
    Generates random sentences using a Markov chain from a given text file.

    :param filename: The name of the text file containing the corpus.
    :param sentence_count: The number of sentences to generate.
    :param sentence_length: The length of each sentence.
    :return: A string containing the generated sentences.
    """
    with open(filename, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as file:
        text = file.read().lower()

    words = re.findall(r'\b\w+\b|[.!?]', text)
    
    markov_chain = defaultdict(list)
    for i in range(len(words) - 1):
        current_word = words[i]
        next_word = words[i + 1]
        markov_chain[current_word].append(next_word)

    def generate_sentence(markov_chain, sentence_length):
        starting_words = [word for word in markov_chain.keys() if word not in '.!?']
        current_word = random.choice(starting_words)
        sentence = [current_word.capitalize()]
        
        while len(sentence) < sentence_length:
            next_possible_words = markov_chain.get(current_word, [])
            if not next_possible_words:
                break
            current_word = random.choice(next_possible_words)
            sentence.append(current_word)
            if current_word in '.!?':
                sentence.append(random.choice(starting_words).capitalize())

        if sentence[-1] not in '.!?':
            sentence.append(random.choice('.!?'))

        return ' '.join(sentence)

    sentences = [generate_sentence(markov_chain, sentence_length) for _ in range(sentence_count)]
    return '\n'.join(sentences)

# Example usage
filename = "corpus.txt"
sentence_count = 3
sentence_length = 10
print(generate_sentences(filename, sentence_count, sentence_length))

The JSON file contains nested objects and arrays representing various user actions. Your goal is to write a Python script that reads this JSON file, flattens its nested structure, and writes the flattened data to a new JSON file named `flattened_data.json`. Your script must handle potential errors such as missing files or invalid JSON format.

**Sample Input:**
```json
{
    "user": {
        "id": 123,
        "name": "John Doe",
        "actions": [
            {"type": "click", "element": "button"},
            {"type": "hover", "element": "menu"}
        ]
    }
}
```

**Sample Output:**
```json
{
    "user_id": 123,
    "user_name": "John Doe",
    "user_actions_0_type": "click",
    "user_actions_0_element": "button",
    "user_actions_1_type": "hover",
    "user_actions_1_element": "menu"
}
```

```python
import json
import os

def flatten_json(y):
    """Flatten a nested JSON dictionary into a single-level dictionary."""
    out = {}

    def flatten(x, name=''):
        """Recursively flatten a nested dictionary or list."""
        if isinstance(x, dict):
            for a in x:
                flatten(x[a], name + a + '_')
        elif isinstance(x, list):
            for i, a in enumerate(x):
                flatten(a, name + str(i) + '_')
        else:
            out[name[:-1]] = x

    flatten(y)
    return out

def read_json_file(file_path):
    """Read and parse a JSON file, handling file not found and JSON decode errors."""
    try:
        with open(file_path, 'r') as file:
            return json.load(file)
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"Error: The file '{file_path}' was not found.")
    except json.JSONDecodeError:
        print(f"Error: The file '{file_path}' contains invalid JSON.")

def write_json_file(file_path, data):
    """Write a dictionary to a JSON file, handling I/O errors."""
    try:
        with open(file_path, 'w') as file:
            json.dump(data, file, indent=4)
    except IOError:
        print(f"Error: Could not write to file '{file_path}'.")

def main():
    """Main function to read, flatten, and write JSON data."""
    input_file = 'user_data.json'
    output_file = 'flattened_data.json'

    data = read_json_file(input_file)
    if data is None:
        return

    flattened_data = flatten_json(data)
    write_json_file(output_file, flattened_data)

    print(f"Flattened data successfully written to '{output_file}'.")

if __name__ == '__main__':
    main()

</think>To solve the problem, we need to find the number of distinct equilateral triangles with side length 1 in a set \( S \) of points inside an equilateral triangle \( ABC \) with side length \( N = 10 \). The points in \( S \) are defined by the vector equation \(\overrightarrow{AM} = \frac{1}{N} \cdot (n \cdot \overrightarrow{AB} + m \cdot \overrightarrow{AC})\) with integer coordinates \( n, m \) such that \( 0 \leq n, m \leq N \) and \( n + m \leq N \). The coloring constraints are:
1. No point on edge \( AB \) (where \( m = 0 \)) is blue.
2. No point on edge \( AC \) (where \( n = 0 \)) is white.
3. No point on edge \( BC \) (where \( n + m = N \)) is red.

### Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Grid Structure**: The points in \( S \) form a triangular lattice inside \( ABC \). Each point can be identified by coordinates \((n, m)\).

2. **Coloring Constraints**: 
   - Points on edge \( AB \) (where \( m = 0 \)) cannot be blue.
   - Points on edge \( AC \) (where \( n = 0 \)) cannot be white.
   - Points on edge \( BC \) (where \( n + m = N \)) cannot be red.

- The class should have an initializer that accepts two parameters: `width` and `height`.
- Both `width` and `height` should be positive numbers. If a non-positive number is provided, raise a `ValueError`.
- Implement properties for `width` and `height` with appropriate validation.
- Implement a read-only property `area` that returns the area of the rectangle.
- Implement a read-only property `perimeter` that returns the perimeter of the rectangle.
- Implement a method `resize(factor)` that resizes the rectangle by multiplying both `width` and `height` by `factor`. If `factor` is non-positive, raise a `ValueError`.
- Implement a `__str__` method that returns a string representation of the rectangle in the format: `Rectangle(width=<width>, height=<height>)`.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
rect = Rectangle(3, 4)
print(rect)  # Output: Rectangle(width=3, height=4)
print(rect.area)  # Output: 12
print(rect.perimeter)  # Output: 14
rect.resize(2)
print(rect)  # Output: Rectangle(width=6, height=8)
```

```python
class Rectangle:
    def __init__(self, width, height):
        """
        Initializes a new Rectangle instance with the given width and height.
        
        :param width: The width of the rectangle (must be positive).
        :param height: The height of the rectangle (must be positive).
        :raises ValueError: If width or height is not positive.
        """
        self.width = width
        self.height = height

    @property
    def width(self):
        """
        Returns the width of the rectangle.
        
        :return: The width of the rectangle.
        """
        return self._width

    @width.setter
    def width(self, value):
        """
        Sets the width of the rectangle.
        
        :param value: The new width of the rectangle (must be positive).
        :raises ValueError: If the value is not positive.
        """
        if value <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Width must be a positive float")
        self._width = value

    @property
    def height(self):
        """
        Returns the height of the rectangle.
        
        :return: The height of the rectangle.
        """
        return self._height

    @height.setter
    def height(self, value):
        """
        Sets the height of the rectangle.
        
        :param value: The new height of the rectangle (must be positive).
        :raises ValueError: If the value is not positive.
        """
        if value <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Height must be a positive float")
        self._height = value

    @property
    def area(self):
        """
        Returns the area of the rectangle.
        
        :return: The area of the rectangle.
        """
        return self.width * self.height

    @property
    def perimeter(self):
        """
        Returns the perimeter of the rectangle.
        
        :return: The perimeter of the rectangle.
        """
        return 2 * (self.width + self.height)

    def resize(self, factor):
        """
        Resizes the rectangle by the given factor.
        
        :param factor: The factor by which to resize the rectangle (must be positive).
        :raises ValueError: If the factor is not positive.
        """
        if factor <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Factor must be a positive float")
        self.width *= factor
        self.height *= factor

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the rectangle.
        
        :return: A string representation of the rectangle.
        """
        return f"Rectangle(width={self.width}, height={self.height})"
```<#sep#>Consider a rectangular board composed of unit squares that can be completely covered by L-Tetrominoes. If the board has dimensions $m \times n$, find the sum of all possible values of $m+n$ when the total number of unit squares is exactly 80.
