<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing an `Inventory` class for a store management system. The class should support the following operations:

1. **Add Item**: Add a new item to the inventory or update the quantity of an existing item. Each item is identified by its name, has a price, and a quantity.
2. **Remove Item**: Remove a specified quantity of an item from the inventory. If the specified quantity exceeds the available quantity, print an error message. If the item does not exist, print an error message.
3. **Get Inventory**: Return the current state of the inventory as a dictionary.
4. **Get Total Value**: Calculate and return the total value of the inventory, which is the sum of the product of the price and quantity for each item.

**Input/Output Specification**:
- The `Inventory` class will be tested with a series of operations. The operations will be provided as method calls on an instance of the `Inventory` class.
- For each `remove_item` operation that fails due to insufficient quantity or non-existent item, an error message should be printed.
- The `get_inventory` method should return a dictionary where keys are item names and values are dictionaries containing the price and quantity of the item.
- The `get_total_value` method should return a float representing the total value of the inventory.

**Sample Input/Output**:
```python
inventory = Inventory()
inventory.add_item("apple", 0.5, 10)
inventory.add_item("banana", 0.3, 20)
print(inventory.get_inventory())  # {'apple': {'price': 0.5, 'quantity': 10}, 'banana': {'price': 0.3, 'quantity': 20}}
print(inventory.get_total_value())  # 11.0
inventory.remove_item("apple", 5)
print(inventory.get_inventory())  # {'apple': {'price': 0.5, 'quantity': 5}, 'banana': {'price': 0.3, 'quantity': 20}}
print(inventory.get_total_value())  # 8.5
inventory.remove_item("apple", 10)  # Error: Not enough apple in inventory to remove.
inventory.remove_item("orange", 5)  # Error: orange not found in inventory.
```

```python
class Inventory:
    def __init__(self):
        """Initialize an empty dictionary to store items."""
        self.items = {}

    def add_item(self, name, price, quantity):
        """Add or update an item in the inventory."""
        if name in self.items:
            self.items[name]['quantity'] += quantity
        else:
            self.items[name] = {'price': price, 'quantity': quantity}

    def remove_item(self, name, quantity):
        """Remove a certain quantity of an item from the inventory."""
        if name in self.items:
            if self.items[name]['quantity'] > quantity:
                self.items[name]['quantity'] -= quantity
            elif self.items[name]['quantity'] == quantity:
                del self.items[name]
            else:
                print(f"Error: Not enough {name} in inventory to remove.")
        else:
            print(f"Error: {name} not found in inventory.")

    def get_inventory(self):
        """Return the current inventory."""
        return self.items

    def get_total_value(self):
        """Calculate the total value of the inventory."""
        total_value = 0
        for item in self.items.values():
            total_value += item['price'] * item['quantity']
        return total_value

802,<#sep#>You are tasked with writing a Python script that processes a CSV file by normalizing its numerical columns. The script should read the CSV file, apply normalization to each numerical column, and then write the normalized data back to a new CSV file. Normalization is defined as subtracting the minimum value of the column and then dividing by the range (max - min). Your script should handle potential errors, such as the CSV file not existing or containing non-numeric data in a numeric column. The script should also include appropriate comments and docstrings.

**Input:**
- A CSV file path as a string.
- The CSV file may contain both numerical and non-numerical columns.
- The CSV file may be empty or contain parsing errors.

**Output:**
- A new CSV file with the same structure as the input file, but with numerical columns normalized.
- If an error occurs, print an appropriate error message.

**Sample Input:**
```
input.csv
id,name,age,score
1,Alice,25,88
2,Bob,30,92
3,Charlie,22,78
```

**Sample Output:**
```
output.csv
id,name,age,score
1,Alice,0.5,0.3333333333333333
2,Bob,1.0,1.0
3,Charlie,0.0,0.0
```

```python
import pandas as pd
import os

def read_csv_file(file_path):
    """Read CSV file from the given file path.

    Args:
        file_path (str): The path of the CSV file.

    Returns:
        pd.DataFrame: The data from the CSV file read into a DataFrame.

    Raises:
        FileNotFoundError: If the file does not exist.
        pd.errors.EmptyDataError: If the file is empty.
        pd.errors.ParserError: If there are issues parsing the file.
    """
    if not os.path.exists(file_path):
        raise FileNotFoundError(f"The file {file_path} does not exist.")
    
    try:
        data = pd.read_csv(file_path)
    except pd.errors.EmptyDataError:
        raise pd.errors.EmptyDataError(f"The file {file_path} is empty.")
    except pd.errors.ParserError as e:
        raise pd.errors.ParserError(f"Error parsing the file {file_path}: {e}")

    return data

def normalize_data(data):
    """Normalize the numerical columns of a given DataFrame.

    For each numerical column, subtract the column's minimum value and divide by the column's range (max - min).

    Args:
        data (pd.DataFrame): The input DataFrame with numerical columns to normalize.

    Returns:
        pd.DataFrame: The DataFrame with normalized numerical columns.

    Raises:
        ValueError: If no numeric columns are found in the DataFrame.
    """
    numeric_data = data.select_dtypes(include=[float, int])

    if numeric_data.empty:
        raise ValueError("No numeric columns found in the DataFrame for normalization.")

    normalized_data = (numeric_data - numeric_data.min()) / (numeric_data.max() - numeric_data.min())

    # Reattach non-numeric columns to the normalized data
    for col in data.select_dtypes(exclude=[float, int]):
        normalized_data[col] = data[col]

    return normalized_data

def save_normalized_file(normalized_data, file_path):
    """Write the normalized DataFrame back to a CSV file.

    Args:
        normalized_data (pd.DataFrame): The DataFrame with normalized data.
        file_path (str): The file path to save the normalized CSV file.
    """
    normalized_data.to_csv(file_path, index=False)

def main(input_file_path, output_file_path):
    """Main function to read a CSV file, normalize its numerical columns, and write the normalized data to a new CSV file.

    Args:
        input_file_path (str): Path of the input CSV file.
        output_file_path (str): Path of the output CSV file with normalized data.
    """
    try:
        # Read the input CSV file into a DataFrame
        data = read_csv_file(input_file_path)
        
        # Normalize the numerical columns of the DataFrame
        normalized_data = normalize_data(data)
        
        # Write the normalized DataFrame to a new CSV file
        save_normalized_file(normalized_data, output_file_path)
        print(f"Normalized data has been written to {output_file_path}.")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An error occurred: {e}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    # Example usage
    main("input.csv", "output.csv")

5,<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Polynomial` class in Python that supports basic arithmetic operations and evaluation. The polynomial is represented internally as a dictionary where keys are exponents and values are coefficients. Implement the following methods:

- `__init__(self, coefficients=None)`: Initializes the polynomial with a list of coefficients provided in descending order of power. If no coefficients are provided, the polynomial is initialized to zero.
- `__add__(self, other)`: Adds two polynomials and returns a new polynomial representing the sum.
- `__sub__(self, other)`: Subtracts the second polynomial from the first and returns a new polynomial representing the difference.
- `__mul__(self, other)`: Multiplies two polynomials and returns a new polynomial representing the product.
- `evaluate(self, x)`: Evaluates the polynomial at a given point `x` and returns the result.
- `__repr__(self)`: Returns a string representation of the polynomial in a human-readable format.

**Sample Input/Output:**

```python
p1 = Polynomial([3, 0, 2])  # Represents 3x^2 + 2
p2 = Polynomial([1, -1])    # Represents x - 1
print(p1 + p2)              # Output: "3x^2 + 1x + 1"
print(p1 - p2)              # Output: "3x^2 - 1x + 3"
print(p1 * p2)              # Output: "3x^3 - 3x^2 + 2x - 2"
print(p1.evaluate(2))       # Output: 14
```

```python
class Polynomial:
    """
    A class to represent a polynomial function.
    The internal representation is a dictionary where keys are the exponents
    and values are the coefficients.
    """

    def __init__(self, coefficients=None):
        """
        Initialize a polynomial with a list of coefficients.
        Coefficients are provided in descending order of power.

        :param coefficients: A list of coefficients for the polynomial
        """
        if coefficients is None:
            self.terms = {}
        else:
            self.terms = {exp: coeff for exp, coeff in enumerate(coefficients[::-1]) if coeff != 0}

    def __add__(self, other):
        """
        Add two polynomials together.

        :param other: Another Polynomial object
        :return: A new Polynomial object representing the sum
        """
        result = self.terms.copy()
        for exp, coeff in other.terms.items():
            result[exp] = result.get(exp, 0) + coeff
        return Polynomial({exp: coeff for exp, coeff in result.items() if coeff != 0})

    def __sub__(self, other):
        """
        Subtract a polynomial from this one.

        :param other: Another Polynomial object
        :return: A new Polynomial object representing the difference
        """
        result = self.terms.copy()
        for exp, coeff in other.terms.items():
            result[exp] = result.get(exp, 0) - coeff
        return Polynomial({exp: coeff for exp, coeff in result.items() if coeff != 0})

    def __mul__(self, other):
        """
        Multiply two polynomials together.

        :param other: Another Polynomial object
        :return: A new Polynomial object representing the product
        """
        result = {}
        for exp1, coeff1 in self.terms.items():
            for exp2, coeff2 in other.terms.items():
                exp = exp1 + exp2
                result[exp] = result.get(exp, 0) + (coeff1 * coeff2)
        return Polynomial({exp: coeff for exp, coeff in result.items() if coeff != 0})

    def evaluate(self, x):
        """
        Evaluate the polynomial at a given point x.

        :param x: The value at which to evaluate the polynomial
        :return: The result of the evaluation
        """
        result = 0
        for exp, coeff in self.terms.items():
            result += coeff * (x ** exp)
        return result

    def __repr__(self):
        """
        String representation of the polynomial.
        
        :return: A string representing the polynomial
        """
        terms = sorted(self.terms.items(), reverse=True)
        parts = []
        for exp, coeff in terms:
            if coeff == 0:
                continue
            if coeff > 0 and parts:
                parts.append('+')
            if coeff == -1 and exp != 0:
                parts.append('-')
            elif coeff != 1 or exp == 0:
                parts.append(f'{coeff:.1f}' if coeff % 1 else f'{int(coeff)}')
            if exp == 0:
                continue
            if exp == 1:
                parts.append('x')
            else:
                parts.append(f'x^{exp}')
        return ' '.join(parts).replace('+ -', '- ')

<#sep#>You are tasked with creating a regular expression to validate email addresses. The email addresses must conform to the following rules:
- The local part (before the '@' symbol) can include letters (both uppercase and lowercase), digits, dots ('.'), underscores ('_'), percent signs ('%'), plus signs ('+'), and hyphens ('-').
- The domain part (after the '@' symbol) can include letters, digits, dots, and hyphens.
- The top-level domain (TLD) must consist of at least two letters.
- The domain can optionally include a country-specific TLD, which also consists of at least two letters.
- The domain must not start or end with a hyphen, and it must not contain consecutive dots.

Write a function `is_valid_email(email)` that returns `True` if the email address is valid according to the above rules, and `False` otherwise.

**Sample Input:**
```
user@example.com
user.name+tag+sorting@example.com
user-name@example.co.uk
user_name@example.org
user-name@sub-domain.example.fr
user@ex-ample.com
user@example.c
user@.com
user@.com.
user@domain..com
user@domain.c0m
user@domain.-com
user@domain.com.
user@-domain.com
@domain.com
user@
user@domain
user@domain.c0m
user@domain.toolongtld
user.name+tag+sorting@.
```

**Sample Output:**
```
True
True
True
True
True
True
False
False
False
False
False
False
False
False
False
False
False
False
False
False
```

```python
import re

# Regular expression for matching valid email addresses
email_regex = re.compile(
    r'^[a-zA-Z0-9._%+-]+'          # Local part
    r'@[a-zA-Z0-9.-]+'             # '@' symbol followed by domain name
    r'\.[a-zA-Z]{2,}'              # Top-level domain
    r'(\.[a-zA-Z]{2,})?$'           # Optional country-specific domain
)

def is_valid_email(email):
    """
    Validates an email address based on specific rules.

    Parameters:
    email (str): The email address to validate.

    Returns:
    bool: True if the email address is valid, False otherwise.
    """
    return email_regex.match(email) is not None

# Example usage:
test_emails = [
    "user@example.com",
    "user.name+tag+sorting@example.com",
    "user-name@example.co.uk",
    "user_name@example.org",
    "user-name@sub-domain.example.fr",
    "user@ex-ample.com",
    "user@example.c",
    "user@.com",
    "user@.com.",
    "user@domain..com",
    "user@domain.c0m",
    "user@domain.-com",
    "user@domain.com.",
    "user@-domain.com",
    "@domain.com",
    "user@",
    "user@domain",
    "user@domain.c0m",
    "user@domain.toolongtld",
    "user.name+tag+sorting@."
]

for email in test_emails:
    print(f'{email}: {is_valid_email(email)}')
```<#sep#>- Both dimensions are multiples of 2 but not 4, but then the area would be divisible by 4, but maybe not tileable.

Wait, but 6x6 is 36, which is divisible by 4? No, 36 ÷ 4 = 9, which is not an integer. So 6x6 is not divisible by 4, hence not applicable. Wait, back to 80. The area is 80, which is divisible by 4. So the rectangle must be divisible into 2x4 or 4x2 blocks. Therefore, the dimensions must be such that:

- If it's divided into 2x4 blocks, then the number of columns must be a multiple of 4, and the number of rows a multiple of 2.

Or, alternatively, since 2x4 can be arranged either horizontally or vertically, the dimensions must be multiples of 2 and 4 accordingly.

Wait, more precisely, for a rectangle to be divided into 2x4 blocks, its height must be a multiple of 2 and its width a multiple of 4. Alternatively, height multiple of 4 and width multiple of 2. Either way, both dimensions must be even, and at least one of them must be a multiple of 4.

Therefore, combining both possibilities, the necessary and sufficient conditions are that both m and n are even, and at least one of them is divisible by 4.

But earlier, with the 2x40 example, even though 2 is even and 40 is divisible by 4, tiling is not possible because 40 isn't divisible by 3. Wait, no, 40 is divisible by 4, but when arranging 2x4 blocks, the width needs to be a multiple of 4, which 40 is. So in a 2x40 rectangle, you can tile it as 1 row of 2x4 blocks arranged horizontally. Wait, but 2 rows and 40 columns. If each 2x4 block occupies 2 rows and 4 columns, then in 2 rows and 40 columns, you can place 10 such blocks (40 ÷ 4 = 10). Each block is 2x4, so 10 blocks would cover 2x40. Each block requires 2 L-Tetrominoes. So total L-Tetrominoes would be 10 x 2 = 20, which is 80 squares. Hence, the 2x40 is tileable by dividing into 2x4 blocks, each of which is tiled with two L-Tetrominoes. Therefore, my previous concern about 40 not being divisible by 3 was misplaced. Because in this case, the tiling is done with 2x4 blocks, not with L-Tetrominoes directly. Therefore, 2x40 is indeed tileable.

Therefore, the correct condition is that both m and n are even, and at least one of them is divisible by 4.

Therefore, for the problem at hand, m x n = 80, and m and n are even with at least one divisible by 4. So let's list all such pairs.

Factor pairs of 80 (both even and at least one divisible by 4):

First, list all factor pairs of 80 where both factors are even:

(2,40), (4,20), (8,10), (10,8), (20,4), (40,2)

Now, check if at least one of the pair is divisible by 4:

- 2: not divisible by 4; 40: divisible by 4. Yes.

- 4: divisible by 4; 20: divisible by 4. Yes.

- 8: divisible by 4; 10: not divisible by 4. Yes.

- 10: not divisible by 4; 8: divisible by 4. Yes.

- 20: divisible by 4; 4: divisible by 4. Yes.

- 40: divisible by 4; 2: not divisible by 4. Yes.

So all these pairs satisfy the conditions. Thus, the possible pairs are (2,40), (4,20), (8,10), (10,8), (20,4), (40,2). But since m and n are interchangeable, the distinct sums are 2+40=42, 4+20=24, 8+10=18.

Therefore, the sum of all possible values of m + n is 42 + 24 + 18.

Calculating that: 42 + 24 = 66; 66 + 18 = 84.

Hence, the answer should be 84.

**Final Answer**
\boxed{84}
</think>To solve the problem of finding the sum of all possible values of \( m + n \) for a rectangular board of dimensions \( m \times n \) that can be completely covered by L-Tetrominoes with a total of 80 unit squares, we proceed as follows:

1. **Area Check**:
   - The total number of unit squares is 80, which is divisible by 4 (since each L-Tetromino covers 4 squares).
   - Therefore, the number of L-Tetrominoes required is \( \frac{80}{4} = 20 \).

2. **Tiling Conditions**:
   - For a rectangle to be tileable by L-Tetrominoes, both dimensions \( m \) and \( n \) must be even.
   - Additionally, at least one of the dimensions must be divisible by 4.

3. **Factor Pairs of 80**:
   - We list all factor pairs of 80 where both factors are even and at least one is divisible by 4:
     - \( (2, 40) \)
     - \( (4, 20) \)
     - \( (8, 10) \)
     - \( (10, 8) \)
     - \( (20, 4) \)
     - \( (40, 2) \)

4. **Sum of Dimensions**:
   - Calculate the sum \( m + n \) for each valid pair:
     - \( 2 + 40 = 42 \)
     - \( 4 + 20 = 24 \)
     - \( 8 + 10 = 18 \)
   - Note that pairs like \( (10, 8) \) and \( (20, 4) \) and \( (40, 2) \) are the same as \( (8, 10) \), \( (4, 20) \), and \( (2, 40) \) respectively, so we consider each pair only once.

5. **Distinct Sums**:
   - The distinct sums are 42, 24, and 18.
   - Summing these values gives the final result: \( 42 + 24 + 18 = 84 \).

Thus, the sum of all possible values of \( m + n \) is:

\[
\boxed{84}

5,245.<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a function `render_products` for a web application that displays a list of products in a tabular format on a webpage. Each product is represented as an object with attributes `name`, `price`, and `description`. Your function should generate an HTML string that represents these products in a table. The table should have headers "Name", "Price", and "Description". Use a simple template engine to construct the HTML string.

**Input:**
- A list of `Product` objects, where each `Product` object has the following attributes:
  - `name`: A string representing the product's name.
  - `price`: A float representing the product's price.
  - `description`: A string representing the product's description.

**Output:**
- A string containing the HTML representation of the products in a table format.

**Sample Input:**
```python
product_list = [
    Product("Laptop", 999.99, "A high-performance laptop suitable for gaming and work."),
    Product("Smartphone", 299.99, "A compact smartphone with a sleek design."),
    Product("Smart Watch", 199.99, "A stylish smartwatch with health monitoring features."),
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```html
<table border='1'><tr><th>Name</th><th>Price</th><th>Description</th></tr><tr><td>Laptop</td><td>999.99</td><td>A high-performance laptop suitable for gaming and work.</td></tr><tr><td>Smartphone</td><td>299.99</td><td>A compact smartphone with a sleek design.</td></tr><tr><td>Smart Watch</td><td>199.99</td><td>A stylish smartwatch with health monitoring features.</td></tr></table>
```

```python
class Product:
    def __init__(self, name, price, description):
        self.name = name
        self.price = price
        self.description = description

def render_products(products):
    """
    Generates an HTML string representing a table of products.

    Parameters:
    products (list of Product): A list of Product objects.

    Returns:
    str: An HTML string representing the products in a table format.
    """
    html = "<table border='1'><tr><th>Name</th><th>Price</th><th>Description</th></tr>"
    for product in products:
        html += f"<tr><td>{product.name}</td><td>{product.price}</td><td>{product.description}</td></tr>"
    html += "</table>"
    return html

# Example usage
product_list = [
    Product("Laptop", 999.99, "A high-performance laptop suitable for gaming and work."),
    Product("Smartphone", 299.99, "A compact smartphone with a sleek design."),
    Product("Smart Watch", 199.99, "A stylish smartwatch with health monitoring features."),
]

print(render_products(product_list))
```<#sep#>Let \( n \) be a positive integer with at least 6 divisors. Denote the divisors of \( n \) in ascending order as \( 1 = d_1 < d_2 < \dots < d_k = n \). Find \( n \) such that \( n = d_5^2 + d_6^2 \).

<think>
Okay, let's try to tackle this problem. So we need to find a positive integer n that has at least 6 divisors. The divisors are listed in ascending order as d1, d2, ..., dk where d1 is 1 and dk is n itself. The key equation we need is n = d5² + d6². Since there are at least 6 divisors, k must be at least 6. So we need to find such an n where the fifth and sixth smallest divisors squared add up to n.

First, let me recall that the number of divisors of a number relates to its prime factorization. For example, if n is a prime, it has only two divisors, so n must be composite. If n is a product of primes, like p*q, then the number of divisors is (1+1)(1+1)=4, which is still less than 6. So n must have a more complicated prime factorization. Maybe it's a cube of a prime, like p³. Then the number of divisors is 3+1=4, still not enough. If it's p²*q, then the number of divisors is (2+1)(1+1)=6, which meets the requirement. Alternatively, if it's p*q*r, three distinct primes, the number of divisors is (1+1)(1+1)(1+1)=8. So possible candidates could be numbers with three distinct prime factors or numbers of the form p²*q.

So first, let's consider the case where n has exactly 6 divisors. The structure of such a number is either p^5 or p²*q, where p and q are distinct primes. But if it's p^5, the divisors are 1, p, p², p³, p⁴, p⁵. So in this case, d5 would be p⁴ and d6 would be p⁵. Then the equation would be n = (p⁴)² + (p⁵)² = p⁸ + p¹⁰ = p⁸(1 + p²). But n is p⁵, so p⁸(1 + p²) = p⁵. That would require p⁸(1 + p²) = p⁵, which simplifies to p³(1 + p²) = 1. But p is a prime greater than or equal to 2, so this equation can't hold. Therefore, numbers of the form p^5 are out. So the only other possibility for exactly 6 divisors is p²*q. Let's focus on that.

If n = p²*q, where p and q are distinct primes, the divisors in order would be: 1, p, q, p², p*q, n. Wait, let's check that. Let's list the divisors properly. The divisors of p²*q are 1, p, p², q, p*q, p²*q. But when ordered, we need to arrange them in ascending order. Suppose p < q. Then the order is 1, p, q, p², p*q, p²*q. Wait, but is q less than p²? For example, let's take p=2, q=3. Then divisors are 1,2,3,4,6,12. So ordered as 1,2,3,4,6,12. Then in this case, d1=1, d2=2, d3=3, d4=4, d5=6, d6=12. So here, d5=6 and d6=12. Then the equation would be n = 6² + 12² = 36 + 144 = 180. But n is 12, so that's not matching. Wait, so in this case, n is 12, but 12 ≠ 180. So this suggests that for n = 12, the equation is not satisfied.

But maybe there exists another n of the form p²*q where d5² + d6² = n. Let's see. Let's take p=2 and q=5. Then n=2²*5=20. Divisors: 1,2,4,5,10,20. So ordered as 1,2,4,5,10,20. Then d5=10, d6=20. So 10² + 20² = 100 + 400 = 500 ≠ 20. Not equal.

Alternatively, p=2, q=7: n=28. Divisors: 1,2,4,7,14,28. Then d5=14, d6=28. 14² + 28² = 196 + 784 = 980 ≠28.

Not working. How about if p=3, q=2. Then n=3²*2=18. Divisors: 1,2,3,6,9,18. Ordered: 1,2,3,6,9,18. So d5=9, d6=18. Then 9² +18²=81+324=405≠18.

Hmm. Not working. So maybe numbers with exactly 6 divisors don't work. Let's check n=28: divisors 1,2,4,7,14,28. d5=14, d6=28. 14² +28²=196+784=980. Not 28. Doesn't work.

Alternatively, maybe we need a number with more divisors. Let's consider numbers with 8 divisors. The number of divisors is 8. Such numbers can be either p^7, p^3*q, or p*q*r, where p, q, r are distinct primes. Let's check which of these could work.

If n is p*q*r, the number of divisors is 8. The divisors would be 1, p, q, r, pq, pr, qr, pqr. When ordered, assuming p < q < r, then divisors would be 1, p, q, r, pq, pr, qr, pqr. So in this case, d5=pq, d6=pr. Then n = (pq)² + (pr)² = p²q² + p²r² = p²(q² + r²). But n = p*q*r. So we have p*q*r = p²(q² + r²). Dividing both sides by p: q*r = p(q² + r²). So p must divide the left side, which is q*r. Since p, q, r are primes, p must be either q or r. But since p < q < r, p is the smallest prime, so p cannot be equal to q or r. Therefore, this equation would require that p divides q*r, so p divides either q or r, which is impossible since they are distinct primes. So this case is impossible. Therefore, numbers with 8 divisors of the form p*q*r can't satisfy the equation.

Another case is n = p³*q. Then the number of divisors is (3+1)(1+1)=8. So divisors are 1, p, p², p³, q, p*q, p²*q, p³*q. When ordered, assuming p < q. So let's take p=2, q=3. Then divisors: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 24. So ordered as 1,2,3,4,6,8,12,24. Then d5=6, d6=8. Then n=24. Let's compute 6² +8²=36+64=100. 100≠24. Not equal. How about p=2, q=5. Then n=2³*5=40. Divisors:1,2,4,5,8,10,20,40. Ordered:1,2,4,5,8,10,20,40. Then d5=8, d6=10. 8²+10²=64+100=164≠40. Not equal.

p=2, q=7. n=56. Divisors:1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56. Ordered. d5=8, d6=14. 8²+14²=64<#sep#>)...' 是 part of the code we write, but the function declared inside is the function(f)'s declaration.

Thus, the keywords: 

关键字：

var 在 code里有多少 times?

在 line2: var a..., → var一次.

 line3's for loop's var e → var 한번.

 line4의 var d →1次.

所以 'var' appears 3 times.

 其他关键字:

 remarks:

- 'for' appears once.

- 'if' appears three times: line5's if(d===c), the else's if(b.length), and the外部的 if (b.length → total 3.

- 'else' appears once（the else后的 code block） →1.

- 'return' appears once.

- 'this' appears in the 'this' inside the for loop →1.

所以.. the keywords:

var *3,

for *1,

if *3,

else *1,

return *1,

this *1.

These are the keywords除了 the 'function' itself是 the declaration of the external function.

此外, the arrow function中的 '=>': 不计入 as a keyword?

因为 '=>' 是 an operator, not a keyword这个吗, according to ECMAScript. However, the scoring specifies that keywords count as 1 per use. The list of ECMAScript keywords includes ‘in', 'if’, 'else’, 'for’, 'return’, 'var’, etc., but not the arrow operator '=>' is not a keyword, but syntax.

Thus the '=>‘ is not considered as a keyword.故 the arrow function的 part does not affect the keywords count.

 Thus the keywords in the function's code are:

Total:

 var:3 ×1 = 3.

 for:1 × 1 →1.

 if ×3 →3 ×1= 3.

 else ×1 →1.

 return:1.

 this:1.

 so total keywords count是3+1+3+1+1+1 =9.

总的 score is the sum of all variables (26) and keywords (9), totaling 35.

Additionally, we have the bonus for if f being undefined<#sep#>ASSIGN_XOR : ^=

0.00<#sep#>Implement a Python class `Hangman` for a simple game where the player attempts to guess a word by suggesting letters. The game randomly selects a word from a given list. The player has a limited number of attempts (6) to guess the word. The class should include the following methods:

- `__init__(self, word_list)`: Initializes the game with a random word from the provided list.
- `display_state(self)`: Displays the current state of the game, including the guessed letters and remaining attempts.
- `guess_letter(self, letter)`: Processes a letter guess and updates the game state accordingly. Returns `True` if the game is over (either won or lost), otherwise `False`.
- `is_game_over(self)`: Returns `True` if the game is over (either won or lost), otherwise `False`.

**Sample Input/Output:**

```
Word: _ _ _ _ _ _ 
Attempts left: 6
Guessed letters: 
Guess a letter: p
Correct guess!
Word: p _ _ _ _ _ 
Attempts left: 6
Guessed letters: p 
Guess a letter: y
Correct guess!
Word: p y _ _ _ _ 
Attempts left: 6
Guessed letters: p y 
Guess a letter: t
Incorrect guess.
Word: p y _ _ _ _ 
Attempts left: 5
Guessed letters: p t y 
Guess a letter: h
Incorrect guess.
Word: p y _ _ _ _ 
Attempts left: 4
Guessed letters: h p t y 
Guess a letter: o
Correct guess!
Word: p y t h o _ 
Attempts left: 4
Guessed letters: h o p t y 
Guess a letter: n
Correct guess!
Congratulations! You've won!
```

```python
import random

class Hangman:
    def __init__(self, word_list):
        """
        Initializes the game with a random word from the provided list.
        """
        self.word = random.choice(word_list).lower()
        self.guessed_letters = set()
        self.remaining_attempts = 6
        self.current_state = ['_'] * len(self.word)
    
    def display_state(self):
        """
        Displays the current state of the game, including the guessed letters and remaining attempts.
        """
        print(f"Word: {' '.join(self.current_state)}")
        print(f"Attempts left: {self.remaining_attempts}")
        print(f"Guessed letters: {' '.join(sorted(self.guessed_letters))}")
    
    def guess_letter(self, letter):
        """
        Processes a letter guess and updates the game state accordingly.
        Returns True if the game is over (either won or lost), otherwise False.
        """
        letter = letter.lower()
        if len(letter) != 1 or not letter.isalpha():
            print("Please enter a single valid letter.")
            return False
        if letter in self.guessed_letters:
            print("You have already guessed that letter.")
            return False
        
        self.guessed_letters.add(letter)
        
        if letter in self.word:
            for index, char in enumerate(self.word):
                if char == letter:
                    self.current_state[index] = letter
            print("Correct guess!")
            
            if '_' not in self.current_state:
                self.display_state()
                print("Congratulations! You've won!")
                return True
        else:
            self.remaining_attempts -= 1
            print("Incorrect guess.")
            
            if self.remaining_attempts == 0:
                self.display_state()
                print(f"Game over! The word was '{self.word}'.")
                return True
        return False
    
    def is_game_over(self):
        """
        Returns True if the game is over (either won or lost), otherwise False.
        """
        return self.remaining_attempts == 0 or '_' not in self.current_state

# Example usage
word_list = ['python', 'hangman', 'challenge', 'developer']
game = Hangman(word_list)
game.display_state()

while not game.is_game_over():
    guess = input("Guess a letter: ")
    game.guess_letter(guess)
    game.display_state()

For<#sep#>You are tasked with writing a Python script that reads a JSON configuration file and updates a specified key with a new value. The script should accept three command-line arguments: the key to update, the new value, and the filename of the JSON file. The script must handle errors gracefully, such as missing files or invalid JSON format, and ensure that the file is saved correctly.

**Input:**
- The script is executed from the command line with three arguments:
  1. `<key>`: The key in the JSON file to be updated.
  2. `<new_value>`: The new value to assign to the key.
  3. `<filename>`: The name of the JSON file to be updated.

**Output:**
- If the key exists in the JSON file, it should be updated with the new value, and a confirmation message should be printed.
- If the key does not exist, it should be added to the JSON file with the new value, and a warning message should be printed.
- If the file does not exist, an error message should be printed.
- If the file is not a valid JSON file, an error message should be printed.
- If any other unexpected error occurs, an error message should be printed.

**Sample Input:**
```
python script.py "username" "newuser" "config.json"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Updated key 'username' to 'newuser' in config.json.
```

```python
import json
import sys

def update_json_config(key, new_value, filename='config.json'):
    """
    Updates a key-value pair in a JSON configuration file.

    Parameters:
    key (str): The key to update in the JSON file.
    new_value (str): The new value to assign to the key.
    filename (str): The name of the JSON file to update.

    Returns:
    None
    """
    try:
        with open(filename, 'r') as file:
            config_data = json.load(file)

        if key in config_data:
            config_data[key] = new_value
        else:
            print(f"Warning: Key '{key}' not found in the JSON file. Adding new key with the value.")
            config_data[key] = new_value

        with open(filename, 'w') as file:
            json.dump(config_data, file, indent=4)

        print(f"Updated key '{key}' to '{new_value}' in {filename}.")

    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"Error: The file {filename} does not exist.")
    except json.JSONDecodeError:
        print(f"Error: The file {filename} is not a valid JSON file.")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An unexpected error occurred: {e}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    if len(sys.argv) != 4:
        print("Usage: python script.py <key> <new_value> <filename>")
    else:
        key_to_update = sys.argv[1]
        new_value = sys.argv[2]
        file_name = sys.argv[3]
        update_json_config(key_to_update, new_value, file_name)

Total sequences without restrictions: (N-L)^{N-2}

Subtract sequences missing at least one element (non-leaf nodes). So using inclusion-exclusion:

Number = sum_{k=0 to m} (-1)^k * C(m, k) * (m -k)^{n-2} }, where m=N-L, and n=N. Wait yes, m = N-L, so:

So the number of surjective functions is sum_{k=0}^{m} (-1)^k * C(m, k) * (m -k)^{ (N-2)} }.

Therefore, the formula can be written using inclusion-exclusion.

So the total answer would be:

C(N, L) * [ sum_{k=0}^k_max (-1)^k * C(m, k) * (m -k)^{ (N-2)} ] where m = N-L, and k_max = m. 

Hmm. Now, the problem is, for N up to 1e6, m could be as big as N-1 (since L can be 1, making m = N-1). So for example, if L is very small, then m is large, but the terms in inclusion-exclusion would involve C(m, k) terms which for large m could be too slow.

Wait, but in code, perhaps with modular arithmetic, can compute this formula with some optimizations?

Alternatively, maybe there is a combinatorial formula that can let us compute this without dealing with Stirling numbers.

Wait let me think again. The problem is that when calculating the number of surjective functions from n to m, which is equivalent to m! S(n, m), and S(n, m) can be written using the inclusion-exclusion formula. So, substituting that into the number:

Total number of sequences is the inclusion-exclusion sum.

Thus, the total number would be:

C(N, L) multiplied by the inclusion-exclusion sum for m = N-L and terms up to m.

Therefore, the formula is:

Number Trees = C(N, L) * (sum_{k=0}^m (-1)^k * C(m, k) * (m -k)^{N-2} )

Where m is N - L.

Hmm, but with N up to 1e6, and m could be up to N-1 (for L=1), then how do we compute this efficiently?

The problem is that for each N and L, m = N-L, and we need to compute the sum over k from 0 to m of ... and with m up to 1e6, the loop would take O(m) for each query, which is O(1e6) per test case. Since N is up to 1e6, and constraints aren't given on the number of test cases, but perhaps in the problem it's one test case. Hmm, but even 1e6 operations would be manageable in programming?

( Any four consecutive Fibonacci numbers Fn, Fn+1, Fn<#sep#>Design a Python class named `Car` to encapsulate the properties and behaviors of a car. The class should have the following attributes and methods:

- **Attributes:**
  - `make`: A string representing the manufacturer of the car (e.g., "Toyota", "Ford").
  - `model`: A string representing the model of the car (e.g., "Camry", "Mustang").
  - `year`: An integer representing the year the car was manufactured.
  - `mileage`: An integer representing the current mileage of the car.

- **Methods:**
  - `drive(distance)`: Increases the mileage of the car by a specified distance. If the distance is negative, raise a `ValueError` with the message "Distance driven cannot be negative".
  - `update_mileage(new_mileage)`: Sets the mileage of the car to a new value. If the new mileage is less than the current mileage, raise a `ValueError` with the message "Cannot roll back the mileage".
  - `get_info()`: Returns a string in the format "{year} {make} {model} with {mileage} miles".

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
car = Car("Toyota", "Camry", 2020, 15000)
car.drive(500)
print(car.get_info())  # Output: "2020 Toyota Camry with 15500 miles"
car.update_mileage(16000)
print(car.get_info())  # Output: "2020 Toyota Camry with 16000 miles"
```

```python
class Car:
    def __init__(self, make, model, year, mileage):
        """
        Initializes a new Car instance with the given make, model, year, and mileage.
        
        :param make: str - The manufacturer of the car.
        :param model: str - The model of the car.
        :param year: int - The year the car was manufactured.
        :param mileage: int - The current mileage of the car.
        """
        self.make = make
        self.model = model
        self.year = year
        self.mileage = mileage

    def drive(self, distance):
        """
        Increases the mileage of the car by the specified distance.
        
        :param distance: int - The distance driven.
        :raises ValueError: If the distance is negative.
        """
        if distance < 0:
            raise ValueError("Distance driven cannot be negative")
        self.mileage += distance

    def update_mileage(self, new_mileage):
        """
        Sets the mileage of the car to a new value.
        
        :param new_mileage: int - The new mileage value.
        :raises ValueError: If the new mileage is less than the current mileage.
        """
        if new_mileage < self.mileage:
            raise ValueError("Cannot roll back the mileage")
        self.mileage = new_mileage

    def get_info(self):
        """
        Returns a string containing the car's make, model, year, and mileage.
        
        :return: str - The car's information in the format "{year} {make} {model} with {mileage} miles".
        """
        return f"{self.year} {self.make} {self.model} with {self.mileage} miles"

However, pow here is fixed as N-2 which could be up to around 1e6, so even then, exp = N-2 is manageable.

Wait, wait 1e6 exponents are manageable with exponents done quickly via binary exponentiation. Wait, but when you do it for each term. For example, in the worst case, m=1e6, so there are 1e6 terms. For each term, you have a base of (m -k) = (N-L -k). The exponent is 1e6-2. So using exponentiation takes log2(exponent) operations per base. So that is like 20 steps per base, so 1e6 terms would require 2e7 operations. Which might be acceptable, but not sure.

Alternatively, can we find a precomputed exponent somehow?

Technical Program Manager, Readymade Masala Pouch, How To Tone Hair With Purple Conditioner, Augustinus Bader Face Oil Reviews, Private Cloud Deployment Examples, 2 Bedroom Apartment For Rent In Brampton,<#sep#>Implement a `BankAccount` class in Python that simulates basic bank account operations. The class should support the following functionalities:
- Initialize the account with an optional initial balance. If the initial balance is negative, raise a `ValueError`.
- Deposit a specified amount into the account. If the deposit amount is non-positive, raise a `ValueError`.
- Withdraw a specified amount from the account. If the withdrawal amount is non-positive or exceeds the current balance, raise a `ValueError`.
- Retrieve the current balance of the account.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- Input: `BankAccount(100).deposit(50).withdraw(30).get_balance()`
- Output: `120`
- Input: `BankAccount(100).withdraw(200)`
- Output: `ValueError: Insufficient funds for withdrawal.`
- Input: `BankAccount(100).deposit(-10)`
- Output: `ValueError: Deposit amount must be positive.`

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, initial_balance=0):
        """
        Initialize the bank account with an optional initial balance.
        Raises ValueError if the initial balance is negative.
        """
        if initial_balance < 0:
            raise ValueError("Initial balance cannot be negative.")
        self.balance = initial_balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount into the account.
        Raises ValueError if the deposit amount is non-positive.
        """
        if amount <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive.")
        self.balance += amount
        return self.balance

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount from the account.
        Raises ValueError if the withdrawal amount is non-positive or exceeds the current balance.
        """
        if amount <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")
        if amount > self.balance:
            raise ValueError("Insufficient funds for withdrawal.")
        self.balance -= amount
        return self.balance

    def get_balance(self):
        """
        Retrieve the current balance of the account.
        """
        return self.balance

# Example usage
if __name__ == "__main__":
    # Creating a bank account with an initial balance of $100
    account = BankAccount(100)

    # Depositing $50
    print(f"Balance after depositing $50: ${account.deposit(50)}")

    # Withdrawing $30
    print(f"Balance after withdrawing $30: ${account.withdraw(30)}")

    # Getting the current balance
    print(f"Current balance: ${account.get_balance()}")

    # Trying to withdraw $200 (should raise an error)
    try:
        print(account.withdraw(200))
    except ValueError as e:
        print(e)

    # Trying to deposit a negative amount (should raise an error)
    try:
        print(account.deposit(-10))
    except ValueError as e:
        print(e)

But note that (m -k) Exponent is pow.

So for all k from 0 to m-1 (since when k>m, m-k is negative but terms become zero), each term is built as:

the combination C(m,k), multiplied by (m-k)^pow (which is in the array), multiplied by (-1)^k.

Wait, and the combination C(m,k) can be computed using factorial terms as well?

If we can compute C(m, k) for all k up to m.

Alternatively, precompute factorial and inverse factorial arrays up to N, so that for any m and k up to 1e6, C(m, k) can be computed as:

C(m, k) = fact[m] * inv_fact[k] * inv_fact[m-k] mod MOD.

Thus, for each k in 0..m, compute C(m, k) each in O(1) time.

So, the plan:

Prerequisites:

- Precompute fact[N] where fact[i] = i! mod MOD, up to N=1e6.

- Precompute inv_fact[i] which is (i!)^{-1} mod MOD, mod MOD. 

These can be precomputed once before any test cases, but given that N can be up to 1e6, and assuming that it's a programming contest, we need to precompute these arrays at the start of the code.

Even if the test case comes in, we can precompute these arrays.

Wait, but in programming competition code, typically the code is required to read input, do the computation, output. So precomputing these arrays once is essential.

So the first step in code would be to precompute fact and inv_fact arrays of size up to maximum N (say 1e6 + some buffer).

Once that is done, for each input N and L, we can do:

First, check if L < N, etc.

Then compute m = N - L.

Check if L is valid, etc.

Now compute the combination C(N, L) = fact[N] * inv_fact[L] * inv_fact[N-L] mod MOD.

Then compute pow = N-2.

Then compute the array a_powers where a_powers[a] = a^pow mod MOD for all a from 1 to m. (since in the sum, the term (m-k) will range from 1 to m, as (m-k) is between 1 (when k = m-1) and m (when k=0))

To compute this array:

Initialize a_powers array of size m+1 (so indices 0 to m). 

Wait, for a from 1 to m:

a_powers[a] = pow(a, pow, MOD).

Wait but how? Computing a^pow mod MOD for each a in 1 to m.

But each such computation could take O(log pow) steps. So for m up to 1e6, each with pow up to ~ 1e6 steps, it would be 1e6 * 20 = 2e7 steps. Which may be manageable, but perhaps it's better to precompute the exponents in a faster manner.

Wait, here comes the Fermat's theorem idea.

Wait, since MOD is a prime, and a is less than MOD. So when a and MOD are co-prime (which is true since a is from 1<#sep#> class MetalCodeGen:

<#sep#># Exposure (photography)

﻿
Exposure (photography)
A long exposure showing stars rotating around the southern and northern celestial poles. Credit: European Southern Observatory
A photograph of the sea after sunset with an exposure time of 15 seconds. The swell from the waves appears as fog.

In photography, exposure is the total density of light allowed to fall on the photographic medium (photographic film or image sensor) during the process of taking a photograph. Exposure is measured in lux seconds, and can be computed from exposure value (EV) and scene luminance over a specified area.

In photographic jargon, an exposure generally refers to a single shutter cycle. For example: a long exposure refers to a single, protracted shutter cycle to capture enough low-intensity light, whereas a multiple exposure involves a series of relatively brief shutter cycles; effectively layering a series of photographs in one image. For the same film speed, the accumulated photometric exposure (Hv) should be similar in both cases.

## Photometric and radiometric exposure

Photometric or luminous exposure[1] Hv is the accumulated physical quantity of visible light energy (weighted by the luminosity function) applied to a surface during a given exposure time. It is defined as:[2]

$H_\mathrm{v} = E_\mathrm{v} \cdot t$

where

• Hv is the luminous exposure (usually in lux seconds)
• Ev is the image-plane illuminance (usually in lux)
• t is the exposure time (in seconds)

The radiometric quantity radiant exposure[3] He is sometimes used instead; it is the product of image-plane irradiance Ee and time, the accumulated amount of incident "light" energy per area:[4]

$H_\mathrm{e} = E_\mathrm{e} \cdot t$

where

If the measurement is adjusted to account only for light that reacts with the photo-sensitive surface, that is, weighted by the appropriate spectral sensitivity, the exposure is still measured in radiometric units (joules per square meter), rather than photometric units (weighted by the nominal sensitivity of the human eye).[5] Only in this appropriately weighted case does the H measure the effective amount of light falling on the film, such that the characteristic curve will be correct independent of the spectrum of the light.

Many photographic materials are also sensitive to "invisible" light, which can be a nuisance (see UV filter and IR filter), or a benefit (see infrared photography and full-spectrum photography). The use of radiometric units is appropriate to characterize such sensitivity to invisible light.

In sensitometric data, such as characteristic curves, the log exposure[2] is conventionally expressed as log10(H). Photographers more familiar with base-2 logarithmic scales (such as exposure values) can convert using log2(H) ≈ 3.32 log10(H).

## Optimum exposure

"Correct" exposure may be defined as an exposure that achieves the effect the photographer intended.[6]

A more technical approach recognises that a photographic film (or sensor) has a physically limited useful exposure range,[7] sometimes called its dynamic range.[8] If, for any part of the photograph, the actual exposure is outside this range, the film cannot record it accurately. In a very simple model, for example, out-of-range values would be recorded as "black" (underexposed) or "white" (overexposed) rather than the precisely graduated shades of colour and tone required to describe "detail". Therefore, the purpose of exposure adjustment (and/or lighting adjustment) is to control the physical amount of light from the subject that is allowed to fall on the film, so that 'significant' areas of shadow and highlight detail do not exceed the film's useful exposure range. This ensures that no 'significant' information is lost during capture.

It is worth noting that the photographer may carefully overexpose or underexpose the photograph to eliminate 'insignificant' or 'unwanted' detail; to make, for example, a white altar cloth appear immaculately clean, or to emulate the heavy, pitiless shadows of film noir. However, it is technically much easier to discard recorded information during post processing than to try to 're-create' unrecorded information.

In a scene with strong or harsh lighting, the ratio between highlight and shadow luminance values may well be larger than the ratio between the film's maximum and minimum useful exposure values. In this case, adjusting the camera's exposure settings (which only applies<#sep#>In a virtual game environment, players gather items, each identified by a unique ID, a name, and a description. Implement a Python class named `Item` that supports the following functionalities:
- Initialize an item with its ID, name, and description.
- Retrieve and update the item's ID, name, and description.
- Print the item's details in the format:
  ```
  Item ID: <item_id>
  Name: <name>
  Description: <description>
  ```

**Sample Input:**
```python
item = Item(101, "Sword of Destiny", "A legendary sword forged in the heart of a volcano.")
item.display_details()
item.set_name("Excalibur")
item.display_details()
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Item ID: 101
Name: Sword of Destiny
Description: A legendary sword forged in the heart of a volcano.
Item ID: 101
Name: Excalibur
Description: A legendary sword forged in the heart of a volcano.
```

```python
class Item:
    def __init__(self, item_id, name, description):
        """
        Initializes a new item with an ID, name, and description.
        
        :param item_id: Unique identifier for the item.
        :param name: Name of the item.
        :param description: Description of the item.
        """
        self._item_id = item_id
        self._name = name
        self._description = description

    def get_id(self):
        """
        Returns the item ID.
        
        :return: The item ID.
        """
        return self._item_id

    def set_id(self, item_id):
        """
        Sets the item ID.
        
        :param item_id: New ID for the item.
        """
        self._item_id = item_id

    def get_name(self):
        """
        Returns the item name.
        
        :return: The item name.
        """
        return self._name

    def set_name(self, name):
        """
        Sets the item name.
        
        :param name: New name for the item.
        """
        self._name = name

    def get_description(self):
        """
        Returns the item description.
        
        :return: The item description.
        """
        return self._description

    def set_description(self, description):
        """
        Sets the item description.
        
        :param description: New description for the item.
        """
        self._description = description

    def display_details(self):
        """
        Displays the item's details in the specified format.
        """
        print(f"Item ID: {self._item_id}")
        print(f"Name: {self._name}")
        print(f"Description: {self._description}")

Wait, the sum is exactly the inclusion-exclusion over subsets, leading to the same formula as the number of surjective functions. So the number is (m! S(N-2, m)) which is the same as the inclusion-exclusion formulas.

Therefore, the number of desired Prüfer sequences is indeed (m!) * S(N-2, m)} where m is N-L. 

But how to compute S(N-2, m)?

The problem reduces to computing S(N-2, m), and then multiply by m! and C(N,L) mod MOD.

Stirling numbers of the second kind can be computed using their recursive formula S(n, k) = k*S(n-1, k) + S(n-1, k-1), but for n up to 1e6 and k up to 1e6, a 2D array for Stirling numbers is impossible (memory-wise) since n can be 1e6 and m can be up to 1e6.

S(n, m) = S(n-1, m-1) + m*S(n-1, m).

sum_{k=0 to m} (-1)^k C(m,k) (m-k)^n = m! S(n, m).

Assuming we have precomputed fact and inv_fact arrays at the beginning.

Computing m = N-L.

Compute pow = N-2.

To compute a_powers array of size m+1 where a_powers[a] = a^pow mod MOD, for a from 1 to m.

Wait, let me think for a moment: if we can compute a^pow mod MOD for all a from 1 to m in O(m log pow) time. Let me think in code.

So the exponentiation for each a takes O(log pow) time.

But for exponents up to 1e6, log2(1e6) is 20. So for each a, up to m terms, total time is 20 million steps, which may be manageable, but even in Python, but in C++ it is, assuming that it's O(m log pow).

But let's see:

Let's say for the worst case m = 1e6 and pow is 1e6.

Each exponentiation is about 20 steps (binary exponentiation). 

Each exponentiation would need to multiply say up to 20 terms. 1e6 * 20 =2e7 operations.  That's manageable in C++ in about a second maybe (assuming 1e8 operations per second).

The summation loop would be O(m) steps, and the combination terms are O(1) with precomputed fact and inv_fact arrays.

So, perhaps this approach is feasible.

Thus, the steps in code would involve:

1. Precompute fact and inv_fact arrays up to max_N (since N can be up to 1e6).

2. For input N and L, compute m = N-L.

<#sep#>expose or underexpose the photograph to eliminate 'insignificant' or 'unwanted' detail; to make, for example, a white altar cloth appear immaculately clean, or to emulate the heavy, pitiless shadows of film noir. However, it is technically much easier to discard recorded information during post processing than to try to 're-create' unrecorded information.

In a scene with strong or harsh lighting, the ratio between highlight and shadow luminance values may well be larger than the ratio between the film's maximum and minimum useful exposure values. In this case, adjusting the camera's exposure settings (which only applies changes to the whole image, not selectively to parts of the image) only allows the photographer to choose between underexposed shadows or overexposed highlights; it cannot bring both into the useful exposure range at the same time. Methods for dealing with this situation include: using some kind of fill lighting to gently increase the illumination in shadow areas; using a graduated ND filter or gobo to reduce the amount of light coming from the highlight areas; or varying the exposure between multiple, otherwise identical, photographs (exposure bracketing) and then combining them afterwards in some kind of HDRI process.

### Overexposure and underexposure

White chair: Deliberate use of overexposure for aesthetic purposes.

A photograph may be described as overexposed when it has a loss of highlight detail, that is, when important bright parts of an image are "washed out" or effectively all white, known as "blown out highlights" or "clipped whites".[9] A photograph may be described as underexposed when it has a loss of shadow detail, that is, when important dark areas are "muddy" or indistinguishable from black,[10] known as "blocked up shadows" (or sometimes "crushed shadows," "crushed blacks," or "clipped blacks," especially in video).[11][12][13] As the image to the right shows, these terms are technical ones rather than artistic judgments; an overexposed or underexposed image may be "correct", in that it provides the effect that the photographer intended. Intentionally over- or under- exposing (relative to a standard or the camera's automatic exposure) is casually referred to as "shooting to the right" or "shooting to the left", respectively, as these shift the histogram of the image to the right or left.

## Exposure settings

### Manual exposure

A longer exposure
A shorter exposure

In manual mode, the photographer adjusts the lens aperture and/or shutter speed to achieve the desired exposure. Many photographers choose to control aperture and shutter independently because opening up the aperture increases exposure, but also decreases the depth of field, and a slower shutter increases exposure but also increases the opportunity for motion blur.

'Manual' exposure calculations may be based on some method of light metering with a working knowledge of exposure values, the APEX system and/or the Zone System.

### Automatic exposure

A camera in automatic exposure (abbreviation: AE) mode automatically calculates and adjusts exposure settings to match (as closely as possible) the subject's mid-tone to the mid-tone of the photograph. For most cameras this means using an on-board TTL exposure meter.

Aperture priority mode (commonly abbreviated to Av) gives the photographer manual control of the aperture, whilst the camera automatically adjusts the shutter speed to achieve the exposure specified by the TTL meter. Shutter priority mode (commonly abbreviated to Tv) gives manual shutter control, with automatic aperture compensation. In each case, the actual exposure level is still determined by the camera's exposure meter.

### Exposure compensation

The purpose of an exposure meter is to estimate the subject's mid-tone luminance and indicate the camera exposure settings required to record this as a mid-tone. In order to do this it has to make a number of assumptions which, under certain circumstances, will be wrong. If the exposure setting indicated by an exposure meter is taken as the "reference" exposure, the photographer may wish to deliberately overexpose or underexpose in order to compensate for known or anticipated metering inaccuracies.

Cameras with any kind of internal exposure meter usually feature an exposure compensation setting which is intended to allow the photographer to simply offset the exposure level from the internal meter's estimate of appropriate exposure. Frequently calibrated in stops,[14] also known as EV units,[15] a "+1" exposure compensation setting indicates one stop more (twice as much) exposure and "–1" means one stop less (half as much) exposure.[16][17]

Exposure compensation is particularly useful in combination with auto-exposure mode, as it allows the photographer to bias the exposure level without resorting to full manual exposure and losing the flexibility of auto exposure. On low-end video camcorders, exposure compensation may be the only manual exposure control available.

## Exposure control

A 1/30s exposure showing motion blur on fountain at Royal Botanic Gardens, Kew
A 1/320s exposure showing individual drops on fountain at Royal Botanic Gardens, Kew

An appropriate exposure for a photograph is determined by the sensitivity of the medium used. For photographic film, sensitivity is referred to as film speed and is measured on a scale published by the International Organization for Standardization (ISO). Faster film, that is, film with a higher ISO rating, requires less exposure to make a good image. Digital cameras usually have variable ISO settings that provide additional flexibility. Exposure is a combination of the length of time and the illuminance at the photosensitive material. Exposure time is controlled in a camera by shutter speed and the illuminance by the lens aperture and the scene luminance. Slower shutter speeds (exposing the medium for a longer period of time), and greater lens apertures (admitting more light), and higher-luminance scenes produce greater exposures.

An approximately correct exposure will be obtained on a sunny day using ISO 100 film, an aperture of f/16 and a shutter speed of 1/100 of a second. This is called the sunny 16 rule: at an aperture of f/16 on a sunny day, a suitable shutter speed will be one over the film speed (or closest equivalent).

A scene can be exposed in many ways, depending on the desired effect a photographer wishes to convey.

## Reciprocity

An important principle of exposure is reciprocity. If one exposes the film or sensor for a longer period, a reciprocally smaller aperture is required to reduce the amount of light hitting the film to obtain the same exposure. For example, the photographer may prefer to make his sunny-16 shot at an aperture of f/5.6 (to obtain a shallow depth of field). As f/5.6 is 3 stops "faster" than f/16, with each stop meaning double the amount of light, a new shutter speed of (1/125)/(2·2·2) = 1/1000 is needed. Once the photographer has determined the exposure, aperture stops can be traded for halvings or doublings of speed, within limits.

A demonstration of the effect of exposure in night photography. Longer shutter speeds result in increased exposure.

The true characteristic of most photographic emulsions is not actually linear, (see sensitometry) but it is close enough over the exposure range of about one second to 1/1000 of a second. Outside of this range, it becomes necessary to increase the exposure from the calculated value to account for this characteristic of the emulsion. This characteristic is known as reciprocity failure. The film manufacturer's data sheets should be consulted to arrive at the correction required as different emulsions have different characteristics.

Digital camera image sensors can also be subject to a form of reciprocity failure.[18]

## Determining exposure

A fair ride taken with a 2/5 second exposure.

The Zone System is another method of determining exposure and development combinations to achieve a greater tonality range over conventional methods by varying the contrast of the 'film' to fit the print contrast capability. Digital cameras can achieve similar results (high dynamic range) by combining several different exposures (varying only the shutter speeds) made in quick succession.

Today, most cameras automatically determine the correct exposure at the time of taking a photograph by using a built-in light meter, or multiple point meters interpreted by a built-in computer, see metering mode.

Negative/Print film tends to bias for exposing for the shadow areas (film dislikes being starved of light), with digital favouring exposure for highlights. See latitude below.

## Latitude

Latitude is the degree by which one can over, or under expose an image, and still recover an acceptable level of quality from an exposure. Typically negative film has a better ability to record a range of brightness than slide/transparency film or digital. Digital should be considered to be the reverse of print film, with a good latitude in the shadow range, and a narrow one in the highlight area; in contrast to film's large highlight latitude, and narrow shadow latitude. Slide/Transparency film has a narrow latitude in both highlight and shadow areas, requiring greater exposure accuracy.

Negative film's latitude increases somewhat with high ISO material, in contrast digital tends to narrow on latitude with high ISO settings.

The calculator should interact with the user by prompting for two numbers and an operator. The program should handle invalid inputs gracefully and continue to prompt the user until a valid operation is performed. The calculator should support the following operations:

- **Addition**: `+`
- **Subtraction**: `-`
- **Multiplication**: `*`
- **Division**: `/`

The program should output the result of the operation or an error message if the input is invalid. The calculator should continue to run until the user decides to stop.

**Sample Input:**
```
1
5
3
2
yes
3
4
2
no
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Select operation:
1.Add
2.Subtract
3.Multiply
4.Divide

Invalid Input
Enter choice(1/2/3/4): 1

Let's do next calculation? (yes/no): yes
Enter choice(1/2/3/4): 2
Enter first number: 4
Enter second number: 2
4.0 - 2.0 = 2.0
Let's do next calculation? (yes/no): no
```

```python
def add(x, y):
    """Return the sum of x and y."""
    return x + y

def subtract(x, y):
    """Return the difference of x and y."""
    return x - y

def multiply(x, y):
    """Return the product of x and y."""
    return x * y

def divide(x, y):
    """Return the quotient of x and y, or an error message if y is zero."""
    if y == 0:
        return "Error! Division by zero."
    else:
        return x / y

def calculator():
    """Run a simple calculator that performs basic arithmetic operations."""
    print("Select operation:")
    print("1.Add")
    print("2.Subtract")
    print("3.Multiply")
    print("4.Divide")

    while True:
        choice = input("Enter choice(1/2/3/4): ")

        if choice in ['1', '2', '3', '4']:
            try:
                num1 = float(input("Enter first number: "))
                num2 = float(input("Enter second number: "))
            except ValueError:
                print("Invalid input! Please enter numeric values.")
                continue

            if choice == '1':
                print(f"{num1} + {num2} = {add(num1, num2)}")

            elif choice == '2':
                print(f"{num1} - {num2} = {subtract(num1, num2)}")

            elif choice == '3':
                print(f"{num1} * {num2} = {multiply(num1, num2)}")

            elif choice == '4':
                result = divide(num1, num2)
                print(f"{num1} / {num2} = {result}")

            next_calculation = input("Let's do next calculation? (yes/no): ")
            if next_calculation.lower() != 'yes':
                break
        else:
            print("Invalid Input")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    calculator()

<#sep#> still recover an acceptable level of quality from an exposure. Typically negative film has a better ability to record a range of brightness than slide/transparency film or digital. Digital should be considered to be the reverse of print film, with a good latitude in the shadow range, and a narrow one in the highlight area; in contrast to film's large highlight latitude, and narrow shadow latitude. Slide/Transparency film has a narrow latitude in both highlight and shadow areas, requiring greater exposure accuracy.

Negative film's latitude increases somewhat with high ISO material, in contrast digital tends to narrow on latitude with high ISO settings.

### Highlights

Example image exhibiting blown-out highlights. Top: original image, bottom: blown-out areas marked red

Areas of a photo where information is lost due to extreme brightness are described as having "blown-out highlights" or "flared highlights".

In digital images this information loss is often irreversible, though small problems can be made less noticeable using photo manipulation software. Recording to RAW format can ameliorate this problem to some degree, as can using a digital camera with a better sensor.

Film can often have areas of extreme overexposure but still record detail in those areas. This information is usually somewhat recoverable when printing or transferring to digital.

A loss of highlights in a photograph is usually undesirable, but in some cases can be considered to "enhance" appeal. Examples include black-and-white photography and portraits with an out-of-focus background.

### Blacks

Areas of a photo where information is lost due to extreme darkness are described as "crushed blacks". Digital capture tends to be more tolerant of underexposure, allowing better recovery of shadow detail, than same-ISO negative print film.

Crushed blacks cause loss of detail, but can be used for artistic effect.

• photography — I (New American Roget s College Thesaurus)<#sep#>Implement a simple text-based game of Hangman. The game should randomly select a word from a predefined list of words and allow the user to guess letters to reveal the word. The user has a limited number of attempts (6) to guess the word correctly. The program should handle repeated guesses and provide feedback to the user on the current state of the word being guessed.

**Input:**
- The user will input a single letter at a time.

**Output:**
- The program will display the current state of the word with guessed letters revealed and underscores for unguessed letters.
- The program will inform the user if the guess is correct or incorrect.
- The program will display the number of attempts remaining.
- The program will announce whether the user has guessed the word correctly or if they have run out of attempts.

**Sample Interaction:**
```
Welcome to Hangman!
_ _ _ _ _
Guess a letter: a
Good guess!
_ _ _ _ _
Attempts remaining: 6
Guess a letter: p
Good guess!
a _ _ _ _
Attempts remaining: 6
Guess a letter: p
You have already guessed that letter!
a _ _ _ _
Attempts remaining: 6
Guess a letter: l
Incorrect guess.
a _ _ _ _
Attempts remaining: 5
...
Congratulations! You guessed the word.
```

```python
import random

def hangman():
    """
    Simulates a simple text-based game of Hangman. The game randomly selects a word from a predefined list,
    allows the user to guess letters, and provides feedback on the current state of the word being guessed.
    The user has a limited number of attempts (6) to guess the word correctly.
    """
    # Define a list of words
    word_list = ['apple', 'banana', 'cherry', 'date', 'elderberry', 'fig', 'grape']
    
    # Randomly select a word
    word_to_guess = random.choice(word_list)
    
    # Initialize variables
    attempts_left = 6
    guessed_letters = set()
    current_word_state = ['_'] * len(word_to_guess)

    print("Welcome to Hangman!")
    print(' '.join(current_word_state))
    
    while attempts_left > 0 and '_' in current_word_state:
        # Get user input
        guess = input("Guess a letter: ").strip().lower()
        
        if not guess.isalpha() or len(guess) != 1:
            print("Please enter a single valid letter.")
            continue
        
        if guess in guessed_letters:
            print("You have already guessed that letter!")
            continue
        
        guessed_letters.add(guess)
        
        if guess in word_to_guess:
            # Update the current state of the word
            for i, letter in enumerate(word_to_guess):
                if letter == guess:
                    current_word_state[i] = guess
            print("Good guess!")
        else:
            # Decrement the number of attempts left
            attempts_left -= 1
            print("Incorrect guess.")
        
        print(' '.join(current_word_state))
        print(f"Attempts remaining: {attempts_left}")

    if '_' not in current_word_state:
        print("Congratulations! You guessed the word.")
    else:
        print("Sorry, you ran out of attempts. The word was:", word_to_guess)

hangman()

Now, implementing pow_mod:

Implement using binary exponentiation:

int bin_pow(int a, int b, int mod) {

    int res=1;

    while (b>0) {

        if (b%2==1) res=( (long long) res*a ) %mod;

        a=( (long long)a*a) %mod;

        b =b/2;

    }

    return res;

}

Now, after filling a_pows, compute the sum S:

    sum = (sum + term) % MOD;

}

We are using cookies for the best presentation of our site. Continuing to use this site, you agree with this.<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Product` class in Python. The class should have the following attributes and methods:

- **Attributes:**
  - `name`: A string representing the name of the product.
  - `price`: A float representing the price of the product.
  - `quantity`: An integer representing the quantity of the product in stock.

- **Methods:**
  - `apply_discount(discount_percentage)`: A method that takes a discount percentage as an argument and reduces the product's price by the specified percentage. The discount percentage should be between 0 and 100, inclusive. If the discount percentage is outside this range, raise a `ValueError`.
  - `restock(amount)`: A method that takes a number of units as an argument and increases the product's quantity by that amount. The amount should be non-negative. If the amount is negative, raise a `ValueError`.
  - `sell(amount)`: A method that takes a number of units as an argument and decreases the product's quantity by that amount. The amount should be non-negative. If the amount is negative, raise a `ValueError`. If there are not enough units in stock to fulfill the sell request, raise a `ValueError`.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- Input: `product = Product("Laptop", 1000, 20)`
- After `product.apply_discount(10)`, `product.price` should be `900.0`.
- After `product.restock(5)`, `product.quantity` should be `25`.
- After `product.sell(10)`, `product.quantity` should be `15`.
- Calling `product.sell(16)` should raise a `ValueError` with the message `"Not enough stock to sell 16 units."`.

```python
class Product:
    def __init__(self, name, price, quantity):
        """
        Initialize a new Product instance.

        :param name: The name of the product.
        :param price: The price of the product.
        :param quantity: The quantity of the product in stock.
        """
        self.name = name
        self.price = price
        self.quantity = quantity

    def apply_discount(self, discount_percentage):
        """
        Apply a discount to the product's price.

        :param discount_percentage: The discount percentage to apply.
        :raises ValueError: If the discount percentage is not between 0 and 100.
        """
        if not (0 <= discount_percentage <= 100):
            raise ValueError("Discount percentage must be between 0 and 100.")
        self.price *= (1 - discount_percentage / 100)

    def restock(self, amount):
        """
        Increase the product's quantity in stock.

        :param amount: The number of units to restock.
        :raises ValueError: If the restock amount is negative.
        """
        if amount < 0:
            raise ValueError("Restock amount must be non-negative.")
        self.quantity += amount

    def sell(self, amount):
        """
        Decrease the product's quantity in stock.

        :param amount: The number of units to sell.
        :raises ValueError: If the sell amount is negative or if there are not enough units in stock.
        """
        if amount < 0:
            raise ValueError("Sell amount must be non-negative.")
        if self.quantity < amount:
            raise ValueError(f"Not enough stock to sell {amount} units.")
        self.quantity -= amount

# Example usage:
product = Product("Laptop", 1000, 20)
product.apply_discount(10)
product.restock(5)
product.sell(10)
try:
    product.sell(16)
except ValueError as e:
    print(e)
```<#sep#>]

Dividing both sides by \( p^2 q \):
\[
1 = q (1 + p^2)
\]

This equation implies that \( q \) must be a fraction, which is not possible since \( q \) is a prime number. Therefore, we need to consider another approach.

Instead, let's directly check specific values of \( n \) to see if they satisfy the condition. We start with \( n = 500 \).

The divisors of 500 in ascending order are:
\[
1, 2, 4, 5, 10, 20, 25, 50, 100, 125, 250, 500
\]

Here, \( d_5 = 10 \) and \( d_6 = 20 \). We check if:
\[
n = 10^2 + 20^2 = 100 + 400 = 500
\]

This satisfies the condition \( n = d_5^2 + d_6^2 \).

Thus, the positive integer \( n \) that satisfies the given condition is:
\[
\boxed{500}
\]<#sep#>1e6.

We can precompute fact and inv_fact arrays up to 1e6+5.

In C++, perhaps:

const int MOD = 1e9+7;

const int MAX_N = 1e6+5;

vector<long long> fact(MAX_N);

vector<long long> inv_fact(MAX_N);

void precompute(){

    fact[0] =1;

    for(int i=1; i<MAX_N; ++i){

        fact[i] = ( fact[i-1] * i ) % MOD;

    }

    inv_fact[MAX_N-1] = bin_pow(fact[MAX_N-1], MOD-2, MOD); 

    for(int i=MAX_N-2; i>=0; --i){

        inv_fact[i] = ( inv_fact[i+1] * (i+1) ) % MOD;

    }

}

This will compute fact and inv_fact.

Thus putting all together:

Now, implementing:

Edge case handling:

Now, code:

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

const long long MOD = 1e9+7;
const int MAXN = 1e6 +5;

vector<long long> fact(MAXN);
vector<long long> inv_fact(MAXN);

void precompute(){

    fact[0] =1;
    for(int i=1; i<MAXN; i++){
        fact[i] = fact[i-1] *i % MOD;
    }
    inv_fact[MAXN-1] = bin_pow(fact[MAXN-1], MOD-2, MOD);
    for (int i=MAXN-2; i>=0; i--){
        inv_fact[i] = inv_fact[i+1] * (i+1) % MOD;
    }
}

long long bin_pow(long long a, long long b, long long mod) {
    long long res=1;
    while(b>0){
        if(b%2 ==1) {
            res = res * a % mod;
        }
        a= a * a % mod;
        b/=2;
    }
    return res;
}

long long comb(int n, int k) {
    if (k <0 || k >n ) return 0;
    return fact[n] * inv_fact[k] % MOD * inv_fact[n -k] % MOD;
}

int main() {

    precompute();
    int N, L; cin >> N >> L;
    if ( L >=N || L <1 ){
        cout <<0<<endl;
        return 0;
    }
    int m = N - L;
    if (m > N-2 ){
        cout <<0<<endl;
        return 0;
    }
    long long C_N_L = comb(N, L);

    int pow_exp = N-2;

    vector <long long> a_pows(m +1);
    a_pows[0] =0; // unused
    for (int a=1; a <= m; a ++ ) {
        a_pows[a] = bin_pow(a, pow_exp, MOD );
    }

    long long sum_S =0;

    for (int k=0; k < m; k++ ){
        // current term for this k:
        int current_m_k = m - k;

        // Compute comb(m,k):
        long long c = comb(m, k);
        long long val = a_pows[ current_m_k ];

        long long term = c * val % MOD;
        term = term * ( (k%2 ==0) ? 1LL : MOD-1 ) % MOD;
        sum_S = (sum_S + term) % MOD;

    }

    long long ans = C_N_L * sum_S % MOD;
    cout << ans <<endl;
    return 0;
}
